 succeeded in 213ms:
 180: \Cref{lem:formal-site-neutral-stack-h1}. This invokes object descent, not
 181: only morphism descent: a one-object \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded prestack has
 182: descent data classified by \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-torsor cocycles, and those
 183: torsors are trivial on every relevant slice. Taking the finite coproduct
 184: over nonempty objects and the shared terminal fibre over $\emptyset$ preserves the prestack
 185: condition: over nonempty objects this is the usual finite coproduct of
 186: prestacks in groupoids, while the strict initial object has only the unique
 187: matching datum. This proves \textup{(iii)} without any appeal to a
 188: constant presheaf sheaf condition.
 189: 
 190: For every nonempty proper intersection object, each twisted summand is
 191: connected and each neutral summand is one connected component; there are no
 192: morphisms between distinct summands. Over $\emptyset$ the shared terminal
 193: fibre has the unique lift object over the empty intersection, matching $F(\emptyset)=\{\ast\}$.
 194: Hence
 195: \[
 196: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(U)\cong\Lambda
 197: \]
 198: for every nonempty proper intersection object $U\to a$. Over $a$, the
 199: nonzero summands have empty fibre and the neutral summands indexed by
 200: \(\Lambda_0\) have one object, so
 201: \[
 202: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)\cong\Lambda_0.
 203: \]
 204: These identifications are compatible with the restriction maps, giving \textup{(iv)}.
 205: 
 206: By \Cref{lem:formal-label-sheafification}, applied to the displayed
 207: presheaf \(F\) and the subset \(\Lambda_0\subseteq\Lambda\), the
 208: sheafification of \(F\) has terminal sections indexed by \(\Lambda\). By
 209: \Cref{prop:stackification-components}, stackification identifies
 210: \[
 211: \pi_0(a(\mathfrak P))\cong aF,
 212: \]
 213: so the components over $a$ are indexed by $\Lambda$.
 214: 
 215: For a fixed $\lambda$, take on $U_i$ the object $x_i^{(\lambda,U_i)}$ if $\lambda\notin\Lambda_0$ and the distinguished constant object if $\lambda\in\Lambda_0$. On $U_{ij}$ take the morphism $(0,i,j)$ in the nonzero case and the identity in the zero case. The triple defect is
 216: \[
 217: (0,i,k)^{-1}\circ(0,j,k)\circ(0,i,j)
 218: =
 219: (\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},i,i)
 220: \]
 221: in the twisted case and is $0$ in the constant case. Under the band identification this is exactly $\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}$. Thus the Cech obstruction cocycle of the $\lambda$-component is $\alpha_\lambda$, proving \textup{(v)}.
 222: \end{proof}
 223: 
 224: \begin{theorem}[Construction with prescribed componentwise homological image subgroups]\label{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}
 225: Let
 226: \[
 227: \mathcal U=\{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I}
 228: \]
 229: be a finite good-cover presentation in a pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$ whose nonempty \v{C}ech nerve
 230: \[
 231: N:=N(\mathcal U)
 232: \]
 233: is connected. Equivalently, the geometric realization of the cover nerve is a
 234: paracompact space homotopy equivalent to $|N|$ and all nonempty finite
 235: intersections of members of $\mathcal U$ are acyclic for constant
 236: coefficients. Let $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ be the finite intersection site associated to the cover nerve of
 237: \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}; it includes the strict
 238: initial object $\emptyset$ for all empty intersections, so disjoint cover
 239: members have a pullback in the site even though they do not contribute a
 240: nonzero finite-nerve cochain. Let $A$ be a finite abelian group, and let
 241: $A_{\mathcal U}$ denote the genuine constant sheaf on
 242: $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ whose value on each nonempty basic intersection
 243: is identified with $A$. View $A_{\mathcal U}$ as the automorphism band of
 244: the banded groupoid lift constructed below, and assume
 245: \[
 246: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0.
 247: \]
 248: Use the Leray comparison isomorphism of
 249: \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(iv). Let $\Lambda$ be a finite nonempty set, and let
 250: \[
 251: \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A
 252: \]
 253: be a nonzero homomorphism for each $\lambda\in\Lambda$. Then, in the
 254: typed-expansion model class generated by
 255: \artresult{Typed-expansion model-class interface for the layer chain}, the following typed data
 256: define a one-state $\mathbb L_2/\mathbb L_3$ model \(M\): the one-state
 257: \(\mathbb L_1\)-reduct, the finite intersection site \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\), the
 258: presheaf of local sections \(F_{p,s}\) constructed below, and the banded groupoid lift
 259: prestack
 260: \[
 261: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_{\mathcal U}
 262: \]
 263: with band \(A_{\mathcal U}\). In that generated model there are a state
 264: \(p\) and a distinguished admissible parameter \(r:\mathsf{Ref}_s\) with
 265: \([r]_p=a\), local slice site \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\), and
 266: \(\mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)\), such that the following hold:
 267: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 268: \item
 269: \[
 270: \Vis_{p,s}(r)\cong \Lambda;
 271: \]
 272: \item under the identification in \textup{(i)}, for each $\lambda\in\Lambda$ the corresponding component gerbe $\mathfrak G_\lambda\subseteq \mathfrak L_r$ has component obstruction class
 273: \[
 274: \omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)
 275: \]
 276: satisfying
 277: \[
 278: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda,
 279: \qquad
 280: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda);
 281: \]
 282: \item $\mathfrak P_r$ is essentially surjective on the terminal fibre over $a$ by the vacuous terminal-fibre case
 283: \[
 284: \mathfrak P_r(a)=\varnothing=\mathfrak L_r(a),
 285: \]
 286: and
 287: \[
 288: M,p\Vdash \NoAmalg_s(r);
 289: \]
 290: \item the aggregate quotients satisfy
 291: \[
 292: Q_{\mathrm{lab}}(r)\cong A\Big/\bigcap_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda),
 293: \qquad
 294: Q_{\mathrm{com}}(r)\cong A\Big/\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
 295: \]
 296: \end{enumerate}
 297: In particular, within the fixed finite-nerve and typed-expansion
 298: framework above, every finite family of subgroups of $A$ arising as images of homomorphisms
 299: \[
 300: H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A
 301: \]
 302: is attained as the family of presentation-relative homological image subgroups of the global component sections of one admissible parameter.
 303: \end{theorem}
 304: 
 305: \begin{proof}
 306: Since
 307: \[
 308: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
 309: \]
 310: the universal coefficient sequence reduces to an isomorphism
 311: \[
 312: \eta_A:H^2(N,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A).
 313: \]
 314: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda$, choose the unique class
 315: \[
 316: \omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)
 317: \]
 318: with
 319: \[
 320: \eta_A(\omega_\lambda)=\phi_\lambda,
 321: \]
 322: and choose a normalized simplicial cocycle
 323: \[
 324: \alpha_\lambda\in Z^2(N,A)
 325: \]
 326: representing $\omega_\lambda$, with the alternating ordered-triple extension
 327: of \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}.
 328: 
 329: By \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(i)--(iv), pass to the
 330: finite intersection site associated to the cover nerve $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ appearing in the statement.
 331: The cover $\mathcal U$, its nerve, and the constant-band finite cochain
 332: data are the same, and Leray comparison identifies
 333: $H^2(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U},A_{\mathcal U})$ with $H^2(N,A)$. The derived component obstruction class
 334: used below is therefore the actual derived class corresponding to the
 335: chosen finite-nerve cocycle.
 336: 
 337: Define the presheaf of local sections
 338: \[
 339: F_{p,s}:\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
 340: \]
 341: by
 342: \[
 343: F_{p,s}(U):=\Lambda
 344: \qquad
 345: \text{for every nonempty proper intersection object }U\to a,
 346: \]
 347: \[
 348: F_{p,s}(a):=\varnothing,
 349: \]
 350: and
 351: \[
 352: F_{p,s}(\emptyset):=\{\ast\}.
 353: \]
 354: Restrictions between nonempty proper intersection objects are identities,
 355: and every restriction to $\emptyset$ is the unique map. This is the
 356: presheaf \(F_{\Lambda_0}\) of
 357: \Cref{lem:formal-label-sheafification} with \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\).
 358: That lemma uses the plus--plus description of sheafification, not a
 359: one-step matching-family replacement, and it treats pullbacks equal to
 360: \(\emptyset\) as strict initial objects carrying only the unique section.
 361: Since the nonempty nerve is connected, it gives
 362: \[
 363: \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda.
 364: \]
 365: 
 366: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda$, define a category fibred in groupoids
 367: \[
 368: \mathfrak P_\lambda\to \mathcal C_{\mathcal U}
 369: \]
 370: by the nonzero-summand construction of
 371: \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}. Thus
 372: $\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)=\varnothing$; over every nonempty proper formal
 373: intersection object $W$ its objects are
 374: $x_i^{(\lambda,W)}$ for $i\in I(W):=\{i\in I\mid W\to U_i\}$; its morphism
 375: sets are the sheaf sections $A_{\mathcal U}(W)$, identified with \(A\) on
 376: connected nonempty \(W\); and composition is twisted by the normalized
 377: cocycle $\alpha_\lambda$ under this identification. Over $\emptyset$ the
 378: fibre is terminal because \(A_{\mathcal U}(\emptyset)=0\). The groupoid
 379: laws, strict functoriality, prestack
 380: condition, and genuine constant-band automorphism-sheaf condition are
 381: exactly \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}(i)--(iii), whose banding
 382: uses \Cref{lem:formal-site-constant-sheaf-banding}.
 383: 
 384: Let
 385: \[
 386: \mathfrak P_r:=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak P_\lambda.
 387: \]
 388: As in \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}, this is the fibrewise
 389: coproduct over nonempty objects with the shared terminal fibre over
 390: $\emptyset$.
 391: The verification that this is a fibred prestack, that its restrictions are strictly functorial, that its automorphism sheaves are the genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$, and that the normalized cocycle identities give the groupoid laws is precisely \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model} with $\Lambda_0=\varnothing$. On every nonempty proper intersection object $U\to a$, each summand $\mathfrak P_\lambda(U)$ is connected, while there are no morphisms between distinct summands. Consequently,
 392: \[
 393: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(U)\cong \Lambda
 394: \qquad
 395: \text{for every nonempty proper }U\to a,
 396: \]
 397: and
 398: \[
 399: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(a)=\varnothing.
 400: \]
 401: Over $\emptyset$ the shared terminal fibre gives
 402: $\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(\emptyset)\cong\{\ast\}=F_{p,s}(\emptyset)$.
 403: Hence
 404: \[
 405: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}.
 406: \]
 407: By \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(v)--(vi), the functor
 408: $F_{p,s}$ and the prestack $\mathfrak P_r$ determine a one-state semantic
 409: model $M$ with admissible parameter $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ at its unique state $p$,
 410: with base object $[r]_p=a$, local slice site $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$,
 411: presheaf of local sections $F_{p,s}$, and banded groupoid lift $\mathfrak P_r$ with
 412: genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$. This admissibility is exactly the expansion convention of
 413: \artresult{Typed-expansion model-class interface for the layer chain}; no additional semantic
 414: closure axiom for freely adjoining presheaf or prestack data is being used.
 415: At this point all semantic interfaces required by the statement have been checked: the admissible parameter is $r$; the finite intersection site associated to the cover nerve is $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$; the presheaf of local sections is the displayed $F_{p,s}$; the prestack band is the genuine constant sheaf $A_{\mathcal U}$; the cover $\mathcal U$ is compared with derived cohomology by the Leray isomorphism of \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(iv); the global component sections are the sheafified terminal sections of $F_{p,s}$; and the satisfaction clauses for $\CompSec_s$, $\Sec_s$, and $\NoAmalg_s$ are read from these matching-family and terminal-section data.
 416: By \Cref{prop:stackification-components},
 417: \[
 418: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s},
 419: \]
 420: so the sheafified global sections at $a$ are exactly
 421: \[
 422: \Vis_{p,s}(r)\cong \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda.
 423: \]
 424: This proves \textup{(i)}.
 425: 
 426: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda$, let $\mathfrak G_\lambda$ be the full substack of $\mathfrak L_r$ determined by the global component section $\lambda$. On the cover $\mathcal U$, take the lift objects
 427: \[
 428: x_i^{(\lambda)}:=x_i^{(\lambda,U_i)}
 429: \]
 430: and, on each overlap $U_{ij}$, take the distinguished isomorphism
 431: \[
 432: \phi_{ij}^{(\lambda)}:=(0,i,j):x_i^{(\lambda)}|_{U_{ij}}\xrightarrow{\sim}x_j^{(\lambda)}|_{U_{ij}}.
 433: \]
 434: Then on every triple overlap $U_{ijk}$,
 435: \[
 436: \bigl(\phi_{ik}^{(\lambda)}\bigr)^{-1}\circ
 437: \phi_{jk}^{(\lambda)}\circ
 438: \phi_{ij}^{(\lambda)}
 439: =
 440: (\alpha_{\lambda,ijk},i,i),
 441: \]
 442: which identifies with $\alpha_{\lambda,ijk}\in A$ under the automorphism-band identification. Therefore the \v{C}ech cocycle produced by
 443: \artresult{\v{C}ech obstruction of a component gerbe on an acyclic cover}
 444: is exactly $\alpha_\lambda$, and the component obstruction class of $\mathfrak G_\lambda$ is
 445: \[
 446: \omega_\lambda=[\alpha_\lambda].
 447: \]
 448: This is the stackification-component compatibility clause of \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model} written on the cover $\mathcal U$.
 449: Thus the finite-nerve comparison uses exactly the fixed good cover
 450: $\mathcal U$, the constant band $A$, and the normalized cocycle
 451: $\alpha_\lambda$ supplied by the homomorphism $\phi_\lambda$; the passage
 452: to derived $H^2(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U},A_{\mathcal U})$ is the Leray comparison of
 453: \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(iv).
 454: Since
 455: \[
 456: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\eta_A(\omega_\lambda)=\phi_\lambda,
 457: \]
 458: one gets
 459: \[
 460: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
 461: \]
 462: This proves \textup{(ii)}.
 463: 
 464: Because each $\phi_\lambda$ is nonzero and $\eta_A$ is an isomorphism, each class $\omega_\lambda$ is nonzero. By
 465: \artresult{Derived component classes detect neutrality}, every component
 466: gerbe $\mathfrak G_\lambda$ is therefore non-neutral, and
 467: \artresult{Terminal-fibre nonemptiness and neutrality} gives
 468: \[
 469: \mathfrak G_\lambda(a)=\varnothing
 470: \qquad
 471: (\forall \lambda\in\Lambda).
 472: \]
 473: Hence
 474: \[
 475: \mathfrak L_r(a)=\varnothing.
 476: \]
 477: Since also
 478: \[
 479: \mathfrak P_r(a)=\varnothing,
 480: \]
 481: the stackification functor on the terminal fibre is
 482: \[
 483: (\iota_r)_a:\mathfrak P_r(a)\to \mathfrak L_r(a),
 484: \]
 485: that is, the empty functor $\varnothing\to\varnothing$, and it is essentially surjective. Thus $\mathfrak P_r$ is essentially surjective on the terminal fibre over $a$.
 486: 
 487: Moreover,
 488: \[
 489: \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda\neq\varnothing,
 490: \]
 491: and the cover $\mathcal U$ carries an explicit strict matching family:
 492: choose any $\lambda\in\Lambda$ and put the section $\lambda\in
 493: F_{p,s}(U_i)=\Lambda$ on every member $U_i$. Restrictions between
 494: nonempty proper intersections are identities, and restrictions to
 495: $\emptyset$ are unique, so the family agrees on every pullback overlap.
 496: Hence \Cref{def:existence-levels}(ii) gives
 497: \[
 498: M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r).
 499: \]
 500: On the other hand,
 501: \[
 502: F_{p,s}(a)=\varnothing,
 503: \]
 504: so
 505: \[
 506: M,p\nVdash \Sec_s(r).
 507: \]
 508: Since $r$ is admissible by construction, it follows that
 509: \[
 510: M,p\Vdash \NoAmalg_s(r).
 511: \]
 512: This proves \textup{(iii)}.
 513: 
 514: Finally, \textup{(iv)} follows by applying
 515: \artresult{Label-aware quotient} and \artresult{Common-character quotient}
 516: to the family
 517: \[
 518: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda)
 519: \qquad
 520: (\lambda\in\Lambda).
 521: \]
 522: The last sentence is the same statement rewritten in terms of the images of the chosen homomorphisms.
 523: \end{proof}
 524: 
 525: The preceding construction theorem makes every component gerbe non-neutral. The next theorem removes that restriction and identifies the neutral component gerbes exactly with the zero homomorphisms.

 succeeded in 205ms:
   1: \section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
   2: 
   3: A stack in groupoids has a sheaf of connected components.  A global section
   4: of that sheaf need not be represented by a global object of the stack, but it
   5: does select a maximal subgerbe.  This is Giraud's component-gerbe construction
   6: \cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  The component is represented by a
   7: global object precisely when its gerbe is neutral, or equivalently when its
   8: Giraud \(H^2\)-class vanishes.  These classical facts are the starting point
   9: of the article.
  10: 
  11: Our setting is deliberately finite and presentation-relative.  We fix a site
  12: with terminal object, a presheaf \(F\), an abelian-banded prestack lift
  13: \(\mathfrak P\), and its stackification \(\mathfrak L\).  The connected
  14: components of \(\mathfrak P\) recover \(F\), while those of
  15: \(\mathfrak L\) recover the sheafification \(aF\).  If \(F\) is separated on
  16: the terminal slice and stackification is essentially surjective on the
  17: terminal fibre, a strict matching family without an amalgamation occurs
  18: exactly when \((aF)(a)\) is nonempty and every component gerbe selected by a
  19: section of \((aF)(a)\) is non-neutral.  This is a comparison between a strict
  20: presheaf predicate and standard gerbe theory, not a new classification of
  21: gerbes.
  22: 
  23: The first paper-specific point is a rigidity statement for a particularly
  24: strong kind of prestack presentation.  Suppose one selects a representative
  25: of every presheaf component and requires those representatives to be stable
  26: under pullback as literal equalities with respect to a split cleavage.  Under
  27: slice separatedness, terminal essential surjectivity then forces the
  28: sheafification unit
  29: \[
  30:  F(a)\longrightarrow(aF)(a)
  31: \]
  32: to be surjective.  Conversely, when \(H^1\) of the band vanishes,
  33: surjectivity makes the Isom torsor between a selected representative and an
  34: arbitrary terminal stack object trivial.  The result is a three-way
  35: equivalence between terminal essential surjectivity, bijectivity of the
  36: terminal sheafification unit, and the sheaf gluing condition at the terminal
  37: object.  The proof in \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity} distinguishes the
  38: strict-representative step from the classical \(H^1\)-torsor step.
  39: 
  40: The second point is an explicit finite-label prestack packaging.  Let
  41: \(\mathcal U\) be a finite good cover with connected nerve \(N\), let \(A\)
  42: be a finite abelian group, assume \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\), and prescribe a
  43: finite family
  44: \[
  45:  \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
  46: \]
  47: UCT selects classes in \(H^2(N,A)\), and the standard Cech construction
  48: realizes each class by an \(A\)-banded gerbe
  49: \cite{Breen2006,Moerdijk2002}.  The construction in
  50: \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} assembles these gerbes
  51: into one prestack while controlling four features simultaneously:
  52: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  53: \item the presheaf and sheaf of component labels;
  54: \item terminal essential surjectivity of stackification;
  55: \item the exact set of neutral and non-neutral labels;
  56: \item the evaluation maps and their image subgroups.
  57: \end{enumerate}
  58: The full construction and all four verifications are part of the article.
  59: 
  60: There is a coefficient hypothesis which cannot be suppressed.  For an
  61: ordinary nerve to index the Cech cochain complex with one copy of \(A\) per
  62: simplex, every nonempty finite overlap must be connected and the coefficient
  63: trivializations must be coherent.  On a disconnected overlap \(U_J\), the
  64: section group is \(A^{\pi_0(U_J)}\), information which the ordinary nerve
  65: does not contain.  We impose the connected-overlap hypothesis explicitly in
  66: \eqref{eq:connected-overlap-hypothesis}.  The open-star covers used for the
  67: wedge application have contractible nonempty intersections and satisfy it.
  68: 
  69: We also separate the formal algebra from the realization.  For a class
  70: \(\omega\in H^2(N,A)\), put
  71: \[
  72:  K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}
  73:  \bigl(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}_\omega}A\bigr),
  74:  \qquad Q_\omega=A/K_\omega.
  75: \]
  76: UCT naturality, quotient initiality, character annihilators, and the Ext
  77: kernel are collected in one proposition.  For finitely many component
  78: classes, the two aggregate quotients are
  79: \[
  80:  A/\bigcap_vK_v,
  81:  \qquad A/\sum_vK_v,
  82: \]
  83: and their comparison is the elementary exact sequence
  84: \[
  85:  0\longrightarrow
  86:  \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}
  87:  \longrightarrow
  88:  A/\bigcap_vK_v
  89:  \longrightarrow
  90:  A/\sum_vK_v
  91:  \longrightarrow0.
  92: \]
  93: No independent information-theoretic claim is made for the cardinalities of
  94: these fibres.
  95: 
  96: The finite-group endpoint is correspondingly stated as a realization
  97: corollary.  On the all-subdivision open-star basis of a finite model of
  98: \(\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2\), a nonzero finite abelian group \(G\) is
  99: realized as the comparison kernel for two complementary nonzero component
 100: images exactly when
 101: \[
 102:  d(G)\le2\beta
 103:  \quad\text{and}\quad
 104:  G\text{ is not cyclic of prime-power order}.
 105: \]
 106: The proof combines the prescribed-image prestack with the elementary fact
 107: that the indecomposable finite abelian groups are the cyclic prime-power
 108: groups.  It is not a classification attached to an arbitrary space,
 109: presentation, or empirical model.
 110: 
 111: \subsection*{Inputs, standard steps, and paper-specific steps}
 112: 
 113: The logical order of the construction is
 114: \[
 115: \begin{array}{c}
 116: (\mathcal C,F,\mathfrak P,\mathscr A)\\
 117: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize stackification}\\
 118: \mathfrak L,\ \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF\\
 119: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize pull back over }v\in(aF)(a)\\
 120: \mathfrak G_v,\ [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\\
 121: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize selected finite presentation and UCT}\\
 122: \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_v}:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A,\quad
 123: K_v,\quad Q_v.
 124: \end{array}
 125: \]
 126: The first line is supplied data.  The component gerbe in the third line is
 127: Giraud's standard maximal subgerbe over a component section.  Its neutrality
 128: criterion and transport under a banded equivalence are also standard.  The
 129: last line uses standard Cech comparison and UCT after a finite presentation
 130: has been selected.
 131: 
 132: The article-specific statements concern restrictions and simultaneous
 133: control across this sequence.  The rigidity theorem identifies a strict
 134: representative hypothesis that collapses a possible terminal gluing failure
 135: when \(H^1\) vanishes.  The prescribed-realization theorem constructs one
 136: prestack in which the component presheaf, terminal fibre, neutrality set, and
 137: finite family of UCT evaluations are fixed together.  The wedge result is
 138: then a corollary obtained by combining that construction with finite-group
 139: arithmetic.  It is not presented as a separate classification mechanism.
 140: 
 141: This division also explains the theorem count.  Component-gerbe existence,
 142: neutrality, banded naturality, UCT, duality, subgroup sums and intersections,
 143: and quotient-fibre cardinality are consolidated rather than restated as
 144: independent theorems.  Numbered statements are retained only where they
 145: serve one of three roles:
 146: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 147: \item record the standard component-gerbe package with its exact Giraud
 148:       antecedent;
 149: \item state a paper-specific comparison, rigidity, or simultaneous
 150:       realization assertion;
 151: \item isolate an application boundary or the wedge realization endpoint.
 152: \end{enumerate}
 153: 
 154: There are likewise two distinct uses of cohomology.  The Giraud class
 155: \[
 156:  [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
 157: \]
 158: decides neutrality of the full component gerbe.  After a finite connected
 159: good-cover presentation with constant coefficient group \(A\) is fixed, its
 160: image under
 161: \[
 162:  H^2(N,A)\longrightarrow
 163:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 164: \]
 165: measures only evaluation on integral \(2\)-cycles.  A nonzero Ext class can
 166: be invisible to this map.  The homological image is therefore a quotient
 167: construction applied to a supplied gerbe class, not a complete obstruction
 168: theory for neutrality.
 169: 
 170: \subsection*{Scope and relation to the companion manuscript}
 171: 
 172: The lift data in this article are supplied.  They are not constructed from
 173: teams, logical models, or empirical probability tables.  A bare empirical
 174: model has a canonical split stack lift whose component gerbes are neutral.
 175: The Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction is instead a relative Cech
 176: \(H^1\)-torsor class indexed by a local section
 177: \cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.  The empty-domain argument
 178: in \Cref{thm:empirical-model-boundary} prevents a universal recovery of those
 179: classes from a global-component-section map.
 180: 
 181: The companion manuscript
 182: \cite{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure} uses meet-semilattice sites.  The
 183: overlap with the present pullback-site treatment is as follows.
 184: \begin{center}
 185: \begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.31\textwidth}
 186:                 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\textwidth}
 187:                 >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth}}
 188: \textbf{Topic here} & \textbf{Companion location} & \textbf{Status here}\\
 189: \hline
 190: Matching versus non-neutral components & Theorem 5.20 & Pullback-site
 191: reformulation with the dependency chain displayed\\
 192: Quotient initiality & Theorem 6.13 & Standard finite-nerve UCT clause in
 193: \Cref{prop:uct-aggregate-package}\\
 194: Component obstruction framework & Section 5 & Standard Giraud construction,
 195: cited at theorem level\\
 196: Terminal representative rigidity & No identical statement & Full narrow
 197: no-go proof in \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}\\
 198: Finite-label prescribed realization & No identical package & Simultaneous
 199: control theorem in \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}\\
 200: \end{tabular}
 201: \end{center}
 202: The table identifies overlap rather than claiming separate priority for the
 203: first three rows.
 204: 
 205: \subsection*{Organization}
 206: 
 207: Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} fixes the minimal presheaf and prestack
 208: interface.  Section~\ref{sec:gerbe-obstruction} records the standard Giraud
 209: component package, proves the comparison criterion, and gives the full
 210: terminal-rigidity proof.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-visibility} proves the
 211: connected-overlap, basis-site, and derived comparisons before constructing
 212: the prescribed finite-label prestack.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-images}
 213: collects the UCT and aggregate consequences.  The wedge application and the
 214: empirical-model boundary occupy Sections~\ref{sec:wedge-application} and
 215: \ref{sec:empirical-boundary}.  The separately compiled technical appendix
 216: contains only presentation bookkeeping, extended examples, and the narrow
 217: lower-language boundary argument; the proofs needed to certify the principal
 218: claims are all contained here.
   1: \section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
   2: 
   3: The article starts with supplied finite-site data: a presheaf, an
   4: abelian-banded prestack lift, and its stackification.  Giraud's component
   5: construction then assigns the maximal component gerbe to each global section
   6: of the component sheaf.  The use made of this standard input is narrow: under
   7: slice separatedness and terminal essential surjectivity it compares the
   8: strict presheaf predicate of matching without amalgamation with non-neutrality
   9: of every global component.  Representative-rigidity shows why a strictly
  10: pullback-stable choice of lift representatives is too restrictive in the
  11: basic \(H^1\)-vanishing regime.
  12: 
  13: On a finite good-cover site, the connected-overlap hypothesis is what permits
  14: the ordinary nerve to carry the full constant-coefficient Cech complex.  The
  15: all-subdivision basis comparison identifies the generated site with the
  16: ordinary sheaf theory of the underlying polyhedron.  Within this fixed
  17: setting, the standard Cech-cocycle gerbe construction can be packaged into a
  18: single prestack whose component presheaf, terminal behavior, neutrality
  19: pattern, and evaluation images are prescribed simultaneously.
  20: 
  21: UCT and elementary subgroup arithmetic then yield the aggregate quotients.
  22: For the selected open-star covers of
  23: \(\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2\), the two-component realization criterion is
  24: \[
  25:  d(G)\le2\beta
  26:  \quad\text{and}\quad
  27:  G\text{ is not cyclic of prime-power order}.
  28: \]
  29: This is a sharp corollary of the selected realization, not a new
  30: classification principle for finite abelian groups or gerbes.
  31: 
  32: Bare empirical models provide only the support presheaf and its canonical
  33: split stack, whose component gerbes are neutral.  Their relative degree-one
  34: obstructions are indexed differently and are not recovered by the
  35: component-section map.  A non-split banded lift must therefore be supplied
  36: before the finite-site results apply.
  37: 
  38: The resulting contribution has a definite boundary.  The following parts
  39: are classical inputs:
  40: \[
  41: \begin{array}{ll}
  42: \text{component over }v\text{ is a gerbe}
  43:  &\text{Giraud III.2.1.5.3},\\
  44: \text{neutrality}\Longleftrightarrow[\mathfrak G_v]=0
  45:  &\text{abelian-banded gerbe classification},\\
  46: \text{finite Cech class}\longrightarrow\text{UCT evaluation}
  47:  &\text{Leray comparison and UCT},\\
  48: \text{aggregate exact sequence}
  49:  &\text{subgroup and quotient arithmetic.}
  50: \end{array}
  51: \]
  52: They are stated together so that the hypotheses used by the applications
  53: remain visible, not as separate advances.
  54: 
  55: The two proofs requiring construction beyond those inputs are terminal
  56: rigidity and prescribed simultaneous realization.  The first identifies
  57: where literal pullback stability removes a descent cocycle and where
  58: \(H^1\)-vanishing removes the remaining Isom torsor.  The second constructs
  59: the label presheaf and twisted groupoid fibres, verifies strict restrictions
  60: and the genuine constant band, computes stackification components and
  61: terminal objects, and recovers the selected Cech cocycles.  Their full proofs
  62: make the claimed scope independently checkable within the article.
  63: 
  64: The technical appendix has a limited role.  Its presentation groupoid records
  65: naturality only along specified comparison words and does not assert path
  66: independence.  Its finite computations illustrate the UCT and subgroup
  67: formulas.  Its lower-language example explains why the finite-site lift is
  68: treated as input but does not construct that lift.
  69: 
  70: No crossed-module comparison is pursued: the present arguments begin after a
  71: gerbe \(H^2\)-class and its Cech representative are supplied, and contain
  72: neither the source-side degree-three chain model nor the comparison cochain
  73: required to derive such a class from crossed-module data.

 succeeded in 214ms:
   1: \section{Presentation-comparison bookkeeping}
   2: \label{sec:presentation-bookkeeping}
   3: 
   4: The article deliberately keeps the homological image attached to supplied
   5: finite-site data.  This section records the limited naturality that is useful
   6: when two such presentations have been related explicitly.  It does not assert
   7: path independence for arbitrary zigzags and does not turn the homological
   8: image into an invariant of an unspecified geometric object.
   9: 
  10: Fix a finite abelian group \(A\).  A \emph{finite presentation} is a pair
  11: 
  12: \[
  13:  (N,\omega),
  14:  \qquad N\text{ a finite simplicial complex},\quad
  15:  \omega\in H^2(N,A).
  16: \]
  17: 
  18: Its evaluation homomorphism and image are
  19: 
  20: \[
  21:  e_\omega:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A,
  22:  \qquad K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(e_\omega).
  23: \]
  24: 
  25: The notation suppresses the universal coefficient transformation.  It is
  26: important that the source group remains visible: an equality between image
  27: subgroups can be justified by naturality only after the relevant map on
  28: homology and the relevant map on coefficients have both been specified.
  29: 
  30: We use three elementary comparison moves.
  31: 
  32: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  33: \item A \emph{retri-vialization move} replaces a normalized cocycle
  34:       \(\alpha\) by \(\alpha+\delta b\) on the same nerve.  It fixes the
  35:       class \(\omega=[\alpha]\), hence fixes \(e_\omega\) and
  36:       \(K_\omega\).
  37: \item A \emph{simplicial move} is a simplicial map \(f:N'\to N\), carrying
  38:       \((N,\omega)\) to \((N',f^*\omega)\).
  39: \item A \emph{coefficient move} is an isomorphism
  40:       \(\theta:A\xrightarrow{\sim}A'\), carrying \((N,\omega)\) to
  41:       \((N,\theta_*\omega)\).
  42: \end{enumerate}
  43: 
  44: Let \(\mathscr P_A\) denote the free groupoid generated by chosen invertible
  45: instances of these moves.  Thus a morphism in \(\mathscr P_A\) remembers its
  46: word in the selected comparison data.  Relations are imposed only for
  47: identity moves, inverses, and literal compositions of the underlying maps.
  48: In particular, two different refinement zigzags with the same endpoints are
  49: not declared equal merely because they compare the same pair of nerves.
  50: 
  51: \begin{proposition}[Naturality along a specified presentation comparison]
  52: \label{prop:presentation-comparison-naturality}
  53: Let a morphism in the presentation groupoid be represented by a simplicial
  54: isomorphism \(f:N'\to N\), a coefficient isomorphism
  55: \(\theta:A\to A'\), and any number of retrivialization moves, so that
  56: 
  57: \[
  58:  \omega'=\theta_*f^*\omega\in H^2(N',A').
  59: \]
  60: 
  61: Then
  62: 
  63: \begin{equation}\label{eq:presentation-evaluation-square}
  64: \begin{CD}
  65: H_2(N',\mathbb Z) @>{e_{\omega'}}>> A'\\
  66: @V{f_*}V{\cong}V @A{\theta}A{\cong}A\\
  67: H_2(N,\mathbb Z) @>{e_\omega}>> A
  68: \end{CD}
  69: \end{equation}
  70: 
  71: commutes.  Consequently
  72: 
  73: \[
  74:  K_{\omega'}=\theta(K_\omega),
  75:  \qquad
  76:  A'/K_{\omega'}\cong A/K_\omega.
  77: \]
  78: 
  79: These identifications are functorial in the chosen word of comparison moves.
  80: No equality between the identifications attached to distinct words is
  81: asserted without an additional coherence argument.
  82: \end{proposition}
  83: 
  84: \begin{proof}
  85: The universal coefficient map is natural in the simplicial complex and in
  86: the coefficient group.  If \(z\in H_2(N',\mathbb Z)\), then
  87: 
  88: \[
  89:  e_{\omega'}(z)
  90:  =e_{\theta_*f^*\omega}(z)
  91:  =\theta\bigl(e_\omega(f_*z)\bigr),
  92: \]
  93: 
  94: which is the commutativity of
  95: \eqref{eq:presentation-evaluation-square}.  Since \(f_*\) and \(\theta\)
  96: are isomorphisms, taking images gives
  97: \(K_{\omega'}=\theta(K_\omega)\).  The induced quotient isomorphism is
  98: \(a+K_\omega\mapsto\theta(a)+K_{\omega'}\).
  99: 
 100: A retrivialization changes a cocycle by a coboundary and hence does not
 101: change its cohomology class.  The calculation therefore applies to each
 102: generator.  Naturality under a composite follows by pasting the corresponding
 103: squares.  Because the groupoid is free on the selected moves, this proves
 104: functoriality along the recorded word and nothing stronger.
 105: \end{proof}
 106: 
 107: The same calculation applies componentwise to the family in
 108: \Cref{art-thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.  Sums and
 109: intersections are carried through a coefficient isomorphism, so the two
 110: aggregate quotients in \Cref{art-prop:uct-aggregate-package} are transported
 111: as well.  This is the entire presentation-comparison claim used by the
 112: article.
 113: 
 114: \subsection{Cocycle changes in coordinates}
 115: 
 116: For completeness, choose a total order on the vertex set of \(N\).  A
 117: normalized \(2\)-cochain is a function
 118: 
 119: \[
 120:  \alpha_{ijk}\in A
 121:  \qquad(i<j<k,\ \{i,j,k\}\in N),
 122: \]
 123: 
 124: extended alternately to other orders and by zero to repeated indices.  Its
 125: cocycle condition on an ordered \(3\)-simplex is
 126: 
 127: \begin{equation}\label{eq:appendix-cocycle-identity}
 128:  \alpha_{jkl}-\alpha_{ikl}+\alpha_{ijl}-\alpha_{ijk}=0.
 129: \end{equation}
 130: 
 131: For a \(1\)-cochain \(b=(b_{ij})\), retrivialization gives
 132: 
 133: \begin{equation}\label{eq:appendix-coboundary-change}
 134:  \alpha'_{ijk}
 135:  =\alpha_{ijk}+(\delta b)_{ijk}
 136:  =\alpha_{ijk}+b_{jk}-b_{ik}+b_{ij}.
 137: \end{equation}
 138: 
 139: If \(z=\sum n_{ijk}[i,j,k]\) is a simplicial \(2\)-cycle, then
 140: 
 141: \[
 142:  \langle\delta b,z\rangle
 143:  =\langle b,\partial z\rangle=0.
 144: \]
 145: 
 146: Thus the coordinate calculation already shows that evaluation on homology
 147: is unaffected by the choice of overlap arrows.  Changing local objects adds
 148: another coboundary after conjugating the overlap arrows.  For an abelian
 149: band, conjugation acts trivially on the coefficient sheaf, and the same
 150: calculation applies.  This is the coordinate form of the standard
 151: naturality statement used in the article's component-gerbe discussion.
 152: 
 153: The subgroup generated by the individual coordinates
 154: 
 155: \[
 156:  H_\alpha=\langle\alpha_{ijk}\rangle\le A
 157: \]
 158: 
 159: need not be unchanged by \eqref{eq:appendix-coboundary-change}.  It follows
 160: that the coordinate quotient \(A/H_\alpha\) is not a substitute for the
 161: homological quotient \(A/K_\omega\).  One always has
 162: 
 163: \[
 164:  K_\omega\subseteq H_\alpha,
 165: \]
 166: 
 167: because evaluating \(\alpha\) on a cycle forms an integral linear
 168: combination of its coordinates, but strict containment can occur.  This is
 169: why the article formulates its quotient through the UCT evaluation map.
 170: 
 171: \section{Finite calculations}
 172: \label{sec:finite-calculations}
 173: 
 174: The following computations illustrate the scope and the limits of the
 175: single UCT package in the article.  They are examples, not additional
 176: classification results.
 177: 
 178: \subsection{A cyclic coefficient group}
 179: 
 180: Let \(A=\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z\), let
 181: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^2\), and suppose
 182: \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\).
 183: The homomorphism
 184: 
 185: \[
 186:  \phi(m,n)=2m\pmod 4
 187: \]
 188: 
 189: has image \(K=\{0,2\}\).  Hence
 190: 
 191: \[
 192:  Q=A/K\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z.
 193: \]
 194: 
 195: Writing characters as
 196: 
 197: \[
 198:  \chi_t(x)=\frac{tx}{4}\pmod{\mathbb Z},
 199:  \qquad t\in\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z,
 200: \]
 201: 
 202: the annihilator of \(K\) is
 203: 
 204: \[
 205:  K^\perp=\{\chi_0,\chi_2\}.
 206: \]
 207: 
 208: These are exactly the characters that factor through \(Q\).  The other two
 209: characters distinguish elements within a quotient fibre and therefore do
 210: not descend.
 211: 
 212: Now take two component images
 213: 
 214: \[
 215:  K_1=\{0,2\},\qquad K_2=A.
 216: \]
 217: 
 218: Then
 219: 
 220: \[
 221:  \bigcap_iK_i=K_1,
 222:  \qquad
 223:  \sum_iK_i=A,
 224: \]
 225: 
 226: and the aggregate exact sequence becomes
 227: 
 228: \[
 229:  0\longrightarrow A/K_1
 230:  \longrightarrow A/K_1
 231:  \longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow0.
 232: \]
 233: 
 234: This degenerate endpoint is useful as a check on directions: the labelled
 235: quotient retains the first component's parity, while the common quotient
 236: retains nothing.
 237: 
 238: \subsection{An Ext-only class}
 239: 
 240: Let \(N\) have the homology of \(\mathbb RP^2\), and let
 241: \(A=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z\).  Thus
 242: 
 243: \[
 244:  H_2(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
 245:  \qquad
 246:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z.
 247: \]
 248: 
 249: The universal coefficient sequence yields
 250: 
 251: \[
 252:  H^2(N,A)
 253:  \cong
 254:  \operatorname{Ext}^1(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)
 255:  \cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z.
 256: \]
 257: 
 258: For the nonzero class \(\omega\), the evaluation map has zero domain and
 259: therefore \(K_\omega=0\).  Its homological quotient is all of \(A\), even
 260: though the corresponding banded gerbe is non-neutral.  This example keeps
 261: separate two questions that can otherwise be conflated:
 262: 
 263: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
 264: \item whether the Giraud class vanishes;
 265: \item whether evaluation on integral \(2\)-cycles detects it.
 266: \end{enumerate}
 267: 
 268: The first answer is no and the second answer is also no.  The homological
 269: image records only the second question.
 270: 
 271: \subsection{Two complementary images}
 272: 
 273: Let \(A=(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^2\) with basis \(e_1,e_2\), and take
 274: 
 275: \[
 276:  K_1=\langle e_1\rangle,
 277:  \qquad
 278:  K_2=\langle e_2\rangle.
 279: \]
 280: 
 281: Then
 282: 
 283: \[
 284:  K_1\cap K_2=0,
 285:  \qquad
 286:  K_1+K_2=A.
 287: \]
 288: 
 289: Thus \(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A\), \(Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0\), and the kernel of the
 290: aggregate comparison is all of \(A\).  Both component images are nonzero
 291: and proper.  A map from \(\mathbb Z^\beta\) can realize either image as soon
 292: as \(\beta\ge1\), and the two maps can be placed in separate components by
 293: the construction of \Cref{art-thm:prescribed-component-image-construction}.
 294: 
 295: For a cyclic group of prime-power order, any two nonzero subgroups are
 296: linearly ordered.  Their intersection is therefore nonzero, so this
 297: complementary pattern cannot occur.  For a cyclic group whose order has two
 298: distinct prime divisors, the primary subgroups for the two primes have zero
 299: intersection and generate the whole group.  These elementary cases account
 300: for the exceptional clause in
 301: \Cref{art-thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss}.
 302: 
 303: \subsection{A matrix check}
 304: 
 305: When \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\) and
 306: 
 307: \[
 308:  A\cong\bigoplus_{r=1}^s\mathbb Z/n_r\mathbb Z,
 309: \]
 310: 
 311: a homomorphism \(\phi:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A\) is represented by an
 312: \(s\times\beta\) matrix whose \(r\)-th row is read modulo \(n_r\).  The
 313: subgroup \(K_\omega\) is the column span in \(A\).  This gives a direct
 314: finite check of every quotient in the article:
 315: 
 316: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 317: \item compute the column span for each component matrix;
 318: \item compute intersections and sums inside the invariant-factor model of
 319:       \(A\);
 320: \item quotient \(A\) by the resulting subgroup;
 321: \item check the character description by solving the corresponding modular
 322:       annihilator equations.
 323: \end{enumerate}
 324: 
 325: The calculation is presentation-relative because the choice of basis for
 326: \(H_2\) and the invariant-factor coordinates of \(A\) are part of the input.
 327: Changing either basis transports the matrices by invertible row or column
 328: operations and leaves the abstract image subgroup unchanged through the
 329: specified isomorphism.
 330: 
 331: \section{A narrow lower-language separation example}
 332: \label{sec:auxiliary-separation}
 333: 
 334: This final section explains why the article accepts a presheaf lift as input
 335: instead of claiming that arbitrary lift data arise naturally from a lower
 336: pointwise semantics.  The example is intentionally narrow.  It establishes
 337: only that one constant-free, one-reference-variable language cannot define a
 338: matching-family predicate.  It does not produce a banded prestack and plays
 339: no role in the proofs cited from the article.
 340: 
 341: Let \(L\) be a relational language with one distinguished sort \(R\), no
 342: constants of sort \(R\), no parameters, and formulas having at most one free
 343: variable \(x:R\).  Let \(\mathcal M\) be an \(L\)-structure and suppose a
 344: finite family of elements of \(R^\mathcal M\) forms a single orbit under
 345: \(\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal M)\).  Automorphism invariance gives
 346: 
 347: \begin{equation}\label{eq:orbit-invariance}
 348:  \mathcal M\models\varphi(r)
 349:  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 350:  \mathcal M\models\varphi(r')
 351: \end{equation}
 352: 
 353: for every parameter-free one-variable formula \(\varphi\) and every two
 354: elements \(r,r'\) in that orbit.
 355: 
 356: Attach to each \(r\in R^\mathcal M\) a finite pullback site
 357: \(\mathcal C_r\) and a set-valued presheaf \(F_r\).  This attachment is
 358: external to the language \(L\).  Choose two orbit elements \(r_0,r_1\) so
 359: that:
 360: 
 361: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 362: \item \(F_{r_0}\) has local sections on a cover whose restrictions agree on
 363:       every pullback overlap;
 364: \item \(F_{r_1}\) has local sections on the corresponding cover, but at least
 365:       one pair has unequal restrictions on a nonempty overlap.
 366: \end{enumerate}
 367: 
 368: Such data can be realized on the cover with two members and one nonempty
 369: overlap.  Give \(F_{r_0}\) a singleton on both cover members and on the
 370: overlap, with the unique restriction maps.  Give \(F_{r_1}\) singleton
 371: sets on the cover members, a two-element set on the overlap, and restriction
 372: maps selecting different elements.  In both cases the terminal value may be
 373: empty.
 374: 
 375: Equation \eqref{eq:orbit-invariance} shows that no formula in the stated
 376: fragment distinguishes \(r_0\) from \(r_1\), while the attached presheaf
 377: predicate ``there exists a strict matching family'' does distinguish them.
 378: Therefore that predicate is not definable uniformly in this fragment from
 379: the pointwise \(L\)-structure alone.
 380: 
 381: The restrictions on the language matter.  With two free reference
 382: variables, equality may distinguish tuples by equality pattern.  With named
 383: parameters, a formula \(x=r_0\) isolates the selected witness.  With symbols
 384: that directly encode restriction maps or matching families, the predicate
 385: may be definable by construction.  The example therefore supplies no broad
 386: undefinability theorem.  It supports only the modest interface decision in
 387: \href{run:main.pdf}{Section~\ref*{art-sec:preliminaries}}: the finite site,
 388: presheaf, and banded prestack
 389: are supplied data unless a separate source construction is proved.
 390: 
 391: The article's empirical-model boundary result,
 392: \href{run:main.pdf}{Theorem~\ref*{art-thm:empirical-model-boundary}}, gives a
 393: substantive instance of the
 394: same caution.  A support presheaf canonically yields a split stack, but a
 395: non-split banded lift and its degree-two class remain additional data.
