   1: \section{Component gerbes and terminal rigidity}
   2: \label{sec:gerbe-obstruction}
   3: 
   4: For a stack in groupoids \(\mathfrak E\), let \(\pi_0(\mathfrak E)\) denote
   5: the sheafification of its presheaf of fibrewise isomorphism classes.  If
   6: \(v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak E)(a)\), define the full substack
   7: \begin{equation}\label{eq:component-substack}
   8:  \mathfrak E[v](X)=
   9:  \{x\in\mathfrak E(X):[x]=v|_X\}.
  10: \end{equation}
  11: The equality in \eqref{eq:component-substack} is equality in the component
  12: sheaf, so it is local by construction.
  13: 
  14: The exact ancestry of this construction is classical.  Giraud proves that
  15: the projection of a stack to its sheaf of connected components is a gerbe
  16: over that sheaf, and that pullback along a component section gives the
  17: corresponding maximal subgerbe
  18: \cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  The next proposition records this
  19: standard construction and the two compatibilities used later.  It is an
  20: input, not an originality claim.
  21: 
  22: \begin{proposition}[Standard component-gerbe package]
  23: \label{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}
  24: Let \(\mathfrak E\) be a stack in groupoids on \(\mathcal C\), and let
  25: \(v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak E)(a)\).
  26: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  27: \item The full substack \(\mathfrak E[v]\) is the maximal gerbe over the
  28:       section \(v\).  If \(\mathfrak E\) is banded by an abelian sheaf
  29:       \(\mathscr A\), then \(\mathfrak E[v]\) has the restricted
  30:       \(\mathscr A\)-banding.
  31: \item Its Giraud class satisfies
  32:       \[
  33:       [\mathfrak E[v]]=0\text{ in }H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
  34:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  35:       \mathfrak E[v]\text{ is neutral}
  36:       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  37:       \mathfrak E[v](a)\ne\varnothing.
  38:       \]
  39: \item Suppose \(\Phi:\mathfrak E\to\mathfrak E'\) is a banded equivalence,
  40:       its band map is \(\theta:\mathscr A\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr A'\),
  41:       and its component map carries \(v\) to \(v'\).  Then \(\Phi\)
  42:       restricts to a banded equivalence
  43:       \(\mathfrak E[v]\simeq\mathfrak E'[v']\), and
  44:       \[
  45:       \theta_*[\mathfrak E[v]]=[\mathfrak E'[v']].
  46:       \]
  47: \end{enumerate}
  48: \end{proposition}
  49: 
  50: \begin{proof}
  51: Clause (i) is precisely the component-gerbe construction of
  52: \cite[III, Prop.~2.1.5.3]{Giraud1971}.  Concretely, \(v|_X\) is locally
  53: represented by an object, so \(\mathfrak E[v]\) is locally nonempty; two of
  54: its objects have the same component class and are therefore locally
  55: isomorphic.  Fullness preserves the automorphism sheaves and hence the band.
  56: 
  57: The classification of abelian-banded gerbes identifies equivalence classes
  58: with \(H^2\), with the neutral gerbes representing zero
  59: \cite[Chap.~IV]{Giraud1971}.  On a site with terminal object \(a\), neutrality
  60: is exactly existence of an object over \(a\), proving (ii).  For (iii), the
  61: component compatibility makes \(\Phi\) restrict to the full component
  62: substacks.  Applying \(\Phi\) to local objects and overlap arrows applies
  63: \(\theta\) to their triple-overlap defect cocycle.  This is the standard
  64: naturality of Giraud classification under a banded equivalence
  65: \cite[Tag 06NY]{StacksProject}.
  66: \end{proof}
  67: 
  68: Apply this package to the stackification
  69: \(\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\) of \Cref{sec:preliminaries}.  In view of
  70: \eqref{eq:stackification-components-core}, every
  71: \(v\in(aF)(a)\) determines the component gerbe
  72: \[
  73:  \mathfrak G_v:=\mathfrak L[v]
  74: \]
  75: and, when \(\mathfrak P\) is \(\mathscr A\)-banded, a class
  76: \[
  77:  \omega_v:=[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A).
  78: \]
  79: On a Leray cover, choosing local objects and overlap arrows produces the
  80: usual Cech \(2\)-cocycle; changing either choice changes it by a coboundary.
  81: This is the local representative of the same Giraud class, not a second
  82: obstruction construction \cite{Breen2006,Moerdijk2002}.
  83: 
  84: \subsection*{The standard cocycle in local coordinates}
  85: 
  86: We spell out the preceding sentence because the realization theorem will use
  87: the same coordinates in the opposite direction.  Let
  88: \(\mathcal U=\{U_i\to a\}\) be a cover on which
  89: \(\mathfrak G_v\) has objects \(x_i\in\mathfrak G_v(U_i)\).  After refining
  90: the cover when necessary, choose isomorphisms
  91: \[
  92:  g_{ij}:x_i|_{U_{ij}}\longrightarrow x_j|_{U_{ij}},
  93:  \qquad
  94:  g_{ii}=1,\quad g_{ji}=g_{ij}^{-1}.
  95: \]
  96: On a triple overlap \(U_{ijk}\), the two arrows from \(x_i\) to \(x_k\)
  97: need not agree.  Their ratio is an automorphism of \(x_i\):
  98: \begin{equation}\label{eq:giraud-triple-defect}
  99:  c_{ijk}
 100:  :=g_{ik}^{-1}g_{jk}g_{ij}
 101:  \in\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(x_i)(U_{ijk})
 102:  \cong\mathscr A(U_{ijk}).
 103: \end{equation}
 104: The last identification is the chosen banding.  We write the band
 105: additively.  Comparing the five composites around a quadruple overlap gives
 106: \begin{equation}\label{eq:giraud-cocycle-equation}
 107:  c_{jkl}-c_{ikl}+c_{ijl}-c_{ijk}=0.
 108: \end{equation}
 109: Thus \(c=(c_{ijk})\) is a Cech \(2\)-cocycle.
 110: 
 111: There are two relevant changes of choice.  First, keep the local objects
 112: fixed and replace the overlap arrows by
 113: \[
 114:  g'_{ij}=b_{ij}\cdot g_{ij},
 115:  \qquad b_{ij}\in\mathscr A(U_{ij}).
 116: \]
 117: Because the band is abelian, substituting in
 118: \eqref{eq:giraud-triple-defect} gives
 119: \[
 120:  c'_{ijk}
 121:  =c_{ijk}+b_{jk}-b_{ik}+b_{ij}
 122:  =c_{ijk}+(\delta b)_{ijk}.
 123: \]
 124: Second, replace \(x_i\) by an isomorphic local object \(x'_i\) and choose
 125: arrows \(h_i:x_i\to x'_i\).  Transporting \(g_{ij}\) by the \(h_i\) gives
 126: overlap arrows for the new objects with the same defect under the band
 127: identification.  Any other choice of transported overlap arrows differs
 128: from these by a \(1\)-cochain and therefore changes the defect by a
 129: coboundary.  Refining the cover pulls the cocycle back.  The resulting
 130: class is consequently independent of all local choices in the Cech colimit,
 131: and the Leray comparison sends it to
 132: \([\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).
 133: 
 134: The neutral case is visible in the same coordinates.  A global object
 135: \(x\in\mathfrak G_v(a)\) restricts to local objects whose canonical overlap
 136: arrows have zero triple defect.  Conversely, if the defect of a chosen local
 137: system is a coboundary, modifying its overlap arrows by the corresponding
 138: \(1\)-cochain makes the defect zero.  Effective descent in the stack then
 139: glues the local objects to a global object.  This is the cocycle-level
 140: description of the neutrality equivalence in
 141: \Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(ii); it is not a replacement
 142: for Giraud classification on a general site.
 143: 
 144: Finally, if a banded equivalence has band map
 145: \(\theta:\mathscr A\to\mathscr A'\), it sends the chosen local objects and
 146: arrows to local objects and arrows whose defect is \(\theta(c_{ijk})\).
 147: This makes the naturality in
 148: \Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(iii) literal on a common
 149: presentation.
 150: 
 151: \begin{theorem}[Presheaf-to-component-gerbe comparison criterion]
 152: \label{thm:gerbe-null-semantics}
 153: Let \(F\) be separated on the slice over \(a\), and let
 154: \(\mathfrak P\) be an abelian-banded prestack lift whose stackification map
 155: is terminally essentially surjective.  Then
 156: \begin{equation}\label{eq:matching-gerbe-criterion}
 157:  \operatorname{MWA}(F,a)
 158:  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 159:  (aF)(a)\ne\varnothing
 160:  \ \text{and}\
 161:  \mathfrak G_v\text{ is non-neutral for every }v\in(aF)(a).
 162: \end{equation}
 163: Equivalently, every \(\omega_v\) is nonzero.  The dependencies are
 164: \[
 165: \begin{array}{c@{\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad}c}
 166:  \operatorname{Match}(F,a)&(aF)(a)\ne\varnothing
 167:  \quad\text{(slice separatedness)},\\[2mm]
 168:  v\in(aF)(a)&\mathfrak G_v\text{ a component gerbe}
 169:  \quad\text{(Giraud III.2.1.5.3)},\\[2mm]
 170:  F(a)\ne\varnothing&\mathfrak L(a)\ne\varnothing
 171:  \quad\text{(terminal essential surjectivity)},\\[2mm]
 172:  \mathfrak G_v(a)\ne\varnothing&\mathfrak G_v\text{ neutral}
 173:  \quad\text{(Giraud classification)}.
 174: \end{array}
 175: \]
 176: \end{theorem}
 177: 
 178: \begin{proof}
 179: By \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i), matching is equivalent to
 180: \((aF)(a)\ne\varnothing\).  By clause (ii) of that proposition and terminal
 181: essential surjectivity, \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\) is equivalent to
 182: \(\mathfrak L(a)\ne\varnothing\).  The latter holds exactly when one of the
 183: full components \(\mathfrak G_v\) contains an object over \(a\).  By
 184: \Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(ii), this is equivalent to
 185: neutrality of at least one component gerbe.  Negating that statement while
 186: retaining nonemptiness of \((aF)(a)\) proves
 187: \eqref{eq:matching-gerbe-criterion}; the cohomological form follows from the
 188: same standard package.
 189: \end{proof}
 190: 
 191: The criterion is a site-theoretic reformulation of the meet-semilattice
 192: comparison in the companion manuscript
 193: \cite[Theorem~5.20]{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure}; it is not an
 194: independent gerbe-classification theorem.  Its role here is to connect the
 195: strict presheaf predicate to the standard component-gerbe construction on a
 196: pullback site.
 197: 
 198: We now give the representative-rigidity argument in full.  This result
 199: isolates a restriction on attempts to realize matching without amalgamation
 200: by an excessively strict lift.
 201: 
 202: \begin{theorem}[Terminal rigidity for representative-rigid lifts]
 203: \label{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}
 204: Let \(F\) be separated on the slice over \(a\), and let \(\mathfrak P\) be a
 205: representative-rigid \(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack lift of \(F\).  Write
 206: \(u:F\to aF\) and \(\iota:\mathfrak P\to\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\).
 207: Then:
 208: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 209: \item terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\) implies that
 210:       \(u_a:F(a)\to(aF)(a)\) is surjective;
 211: \item if \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\), surjectivity of \(u_a\) implies
 212:       terminal essential surjectivity of \(\iota\).
 213: \end{enumerate}
 214: Under the same \(H^1\)-vanishing hypothesis, the following are equivalent:
 215: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
 216: \item \(\iota\) is terminally essentially surjective;
 217: \item \(u_a\) is bijective;
 218: \item every strict matching family on every cover of \(a\) has a unique
 219:       amalgamation in \(F(a)\).
 220: \end{enumerate}
 221: Strict pullback stability of the selected representatives is used in
 222: \textup{(i)}.  The converse \textup{(ii)} uses only the standard
 223: \(\mathscr A\)-torsor of isomorphisms and its \(H^1\)-class.
 224: \end{theorem}
 225: 
 226: \begin{proof}
 227: We separate the proof into the forward descent construction, the converse
 228: torsor argument, and the comparison with strict amalgamation.
 229: 
 230: \medskip
 231: \noindent\emph{Forward descent construction.}
 232: Let
 233: \(\xi\in(aF)(a)=\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\).  By the proof of
 234: \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i), there is a cover
 235: \(\{U_i\to a\}\) and a strict matching family \(t_i\in F(U_i)\)
 236: representing \(\xi\).  Representative-rigidity supplies objects
 237: \[
 238:  y_i=s_{U_i}(t_i)\in\mathfrak P(U_i).
 239: \]
 240: On \(U_{ij}\), strict pullback stability gives literal equalities
 241: \[
 242:  y_i|_{U_{ij}}
 243:  =s_{U_{ij}}(t_i|_{U_{ij}})
 244:  =s_{U_{ij}}(t_j|_{U_{ij}})
 245:  =y_j|_{U_{ij}}.
 246: \]
 247: The identity arrows of these common objects are therefore legitimate
 248: descent arrows.  Their triple composites are identities on the nose, so
 249: stack descent glues the \(y_i\) to an object
 250: \(x_\xi\in\mathfrak L(a)\) with component class \(\xi\).  This is the sole
 251: place where strict pullback stability is used.  With merely
 252: pseudofunctorial representatives, cartesian comparison arrows would enter
 253: and their coherence would have to be controlled separately.
 254: 
 255: The descent datum just constructed is stronger than the datum provided by
 256: equality of component classes alone.  Equality
 257: \([y_i|_{U_{ij}}]=[y_j|_{U_{ij}}]\) would only guarantee local
 258: isomorphisms after further covers.  Equation
 259: \eqref{eq:strict-representative-pullback} instead gives the same object on
 260: the overlap.  Hence the chosen descent arrow is the identity, not a selected
 261: arrow in a nontrivial Isom torsor.  On \(U_{ijk}\) the descent equation is
 262: \[
 263:  1_{y_k}\circ1_{y_j}=1_{y_i}
 264: \]
 265: after the literal identifications of the three restrictions.  No
 266: \(\mathscr A\)-valued \(2\)-cocycle remains to be killed.  This explains
 267: exactly why strict pullback stability, rather than a componentwise choice of
 268: objects, is the forward hypothesis.
 269: 
 270: Assume terminal essential surjectivity.  There is
 271: \(y\in\mathfrak P(a)\) and an isomorphism
 272: \(\iota(y)\cong x_\xi\).  Let \(t\in F(a)\) be the component of \(y\).
 273: The component map of \(\iota\) is \(u\), hence
 274: \[
 275:  u_a(t)=[\iota(y)]=[x_\xi]=\xi.
 276: \]
 277: Since \(\xi\) was arbitrary, \(u_a\) is surjective.  This proves (i).
 278: 
 279: \medskip
 280: \noindent\emph{The converse Isom-torsor argument.}
 281: For (ii), assume \(u_a\) is surjective and take any
 282: \(x\in\mathfrak L(a)\).  Choose \(t\in F(a)\) with
 283: \(u_a(t)=[x]\), and put \(y=s_a(t)\).  The sheaf
 284: \[
 285:  \mathcal I_{y,x}:=
 286:  \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}(\iota(y),x)
 287: \]
 288: is locally nonempty because the two objects have the same component class.
 289: Composition makes it a simply transitive torsor under
 290: \(\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\iota(y))\cong\mathscr A\).  This is the
 291: usual Isom-torsor of a banded stack
 292: \cite[Chap.~III]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}, \cite[Tag 04TU]{StacksProject}.
 293: More explicitly, choose a cover \(\{V_j\to a\}\) and isomorphisms
 294: \[
 295:  h_j:\iota(y)|_{V_j}\xrightarrow{\sim}x|_{V_j}.
 296: \]
 297: On \(V_{jk}\), the ratio
 298: \[
 299:  d_{jk}=h_k^{-1}h_j
 300: \]
 301: is an automorphism of \(\iota(y)|_{V_{jk}}\), hence a section of
 302: \(\mathscr A(V_{jk})\).  The equality of threefold composites gives
 303: \[
 304:  d_{jk}+d_{kl}=d_{jl}
 305: \]
 306: on \(V_{jkl}\), so \(d=(d_{jk})\) is a Cech \(1\)-cocycle representing
 307: the Isom torsor.  If its class vanishes, there are sections
 308: \(e_j\in\mathscr A(V_j)\) with \(d_{jk}=e_k-e_j\).  Replacing
 309: \(h_j\) by \(h_j\cdot e_j\) makes the local isomorphisms agree on overlaps,
 310: and the sheaf condition for Isom glues them to a global isomorphism
 311: \(\iota(y)\cong x\).
 312: 
 313: The hypothesis \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\) makes every such torsor
 314: trivial, so \(\mathcal I_{y,x}\) has a global section.  Thus
 315: \(\iota(y)\cong x\), proving terminal essential surjectivity.
 316: 
 317: \medskip
 318: \noindent\emph{Comparison with strict amalgamation.}
 319: It remains to compare (b) and (c).  Slice separatedness implies that \(u_a\)
 320: is injective: equality after sheafification is witnessed after a cover, and
 321: separatedness at \(a\) returns equality in \(F(a)\).  Thus surjectivity and
 322: bijectivity of \(u_a\) are equivalent.
 323: 
 324: Suppose \(u_a\) is bijective and let \((t_i)\) be a strict matching family
 325: on a cover of \(a\).  It glues in \(aF\) to \(\xi\); choose
 326: \(t\in F(a)\) with \(u_a(t)=\xi\).  Over each \(U_i\), the sections
 327: \(t|_{U_i}\) and \(t_i\) become equal in \(aF(U_i)\).  Such equality is
 328: witnessed after a further cover of \(U_i\), and separatedness at that object
 329: of the slice gives \(t|_{U_i}=t_i\).  Hence \(t\) is an amalgamation, unique
 330: by separatedness at \(a\).
 331: 
 332: Conversely, assume every matching family on a cover of \(a\) has a unique
 333: amalgamation.  Given \(\xi\in(aF)(a)\), the plus--plus representation and
 334: slice separatedness used in
 335: \Cref{prop:finite-site-semantic-interface}(i) produce a strict matching
 336: family representing \(\xi\).  Its amalgamation \(t\in F(a)\) satisfies
 337: \(u_a(t)=\xi\), so \(u_a\) is surjective and hence bijective.  Combining
 338: this equivalence with (i) and (ii) proves (a)--(c).
 339: 
 340: The roles of the hypotheses are now separate.  Slice separatedness upgrades
 341: locally witnessed equality of presheaf sections to strict equality and gives
 342: uniqueness of amalgamations.  Representative-rigidity turns a strict
 343: matching family into identity-valued object descent data.  Terminal
 344: essential surjectivity is used only in the forward passage from a stack
 345: object to a prestack object.  Finally,
 346: \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\) is used only to trivialize the Isom torsor
 347: in the converse.  None of these steps supplies a new classification of
 348: gerbes.
 349: \end{proof}
 350: 
 351: Consequently, on a slice with \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\), matching
 352: without amalgamation cannot coexist with both terminal essential
 353: surjectivity and a strictly pullback-stable representative choice.  This is
 354: a no-go statement for that rigid presentation of a lift; it is not a new
 355: general descent or gerbe-classification principle.

 succeeded in 209ms:
   1: \subsection{Banded groupoid lifts and connected components}
   2: 
   3: The presheaf $F_{p,s}$ records only isomorphism classes of local lift objects. To recover gerbes and cohomology one needs a groupoid-valued lift whose connected components give back $F_{p,s}$ after sheafification. We use the standard prestack, stackification, and banded-gerbe conventions of \cite{Giraud1971,MacLaneMoerdijk1994,Moerdijk2002,StacksProject}. Once an abelian band has been fixed, split lifts always exist; the substantive issue is whether a chosen lift is gluing-sensitive at the terminal fibre.
   4: 
   5: \begin{definition}[Banded groupoid lift and stackification]\label{def:banded-groupoid-lift}
   6: Fix an admissible parameter $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ at a state $p$, and write
   7: \[
   8: a=[r]_p,
   9: \qquad
  10: \mathcal C_a:=\mathcal C_{p,s}/a.
  11: \]
  12: A \emph{banded groupoid lift} for $r$ at $p$ over the pullback-based slice site is a category fibred in groupoids
  13: \[
  14: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_a
  15: \]
  16: such that:
  17: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  18: \item the pre-stackification connected-component presheaf is the restriction of the presheaf of local sections,
  19: \[
  20: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a};
  21: \]
  22: \item for every object $U$ of $\mathcal C_a$ and every $x\in \mathfrak P_r(U)$, the automorphism sheaf
  23: \[
  24: \underline{\mathrm{Aut}}(x)
  25: \]
  26: on $\mathcal C_a/U$ is functorially identified with the restriction of a fixed abelian sheaf $\Band$ on $\mathcal C_a$.
  27: \end{enumerate}
  28: Write
  29: \[
  30: \mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)
  31: \]
  32: for the stackification of $\mathfrak P_r$ in groupoids \cite[Tag 02ZP]{StacksProject}.
  33: \end{definition}
  34: 
  35: \begin{proposition}[Separated split banded groupoid lift]\label{prop:split-banded-groupoid-lift}
  36: Let $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ be admissible at $p$, write
  37: \[
  38: a=[r]_p,\qquad \mathcal C_a:=\mathcal C_{p,s}/a,
  39: \]
  40: and let $\Band$ be an abelian sheaf on $\mathcal C_a$. There is a canonical split banded groupoid lift
  41: \[
  42: \mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}}\to\mathcal C_a
  43: \]
  44: with band $\Band$ and connected-component presheaf
  45: \[
  46: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}})
  47: \cong F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
  48: \]
  49: Explicitly, for every object $U$ of $\mathcal C_a$, the groupoid
  50: $\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}}(U)$ has object set $F_{p,s}(U)$ and
  51: \[
  52: \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}}(U)}(\sigma,\tau)=
  53: \begin{cases}
  54: \Band(U),& \sigma=\tau,\\
  55: \varnothing,& \sigma\ne\tau,
  56: \end{cases}
  57: \]
  58: with composition given by addition in $\Band(U)$. For a morphism
  59: $f:V\to U$ in $\mathcal C_a$, restriction sends $\sigma$ to
  60: $\res_V^U(\sigma)$ and sends an automorphism $b\in\Band(U)$ to its
  61: restriction $b|_V\in\Band(V)$.
  62: 
  63: This split prestack is separated as a prestack in groupoids, in the
  64: sense that every isomorphism presheaf is a sheaf, if and only if the
  65: restricted presheaf of local sections $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is
  66: separated on the slice in the sense of \Cref{def:separated-restriction}.
  67: \end{proposition}
  68: 
  69: \begin{proof}
  70: The displayed rule defines a contravariant pseudofunctor from
  71: $\mathcal C_a$ to groupoids. Since $\mathcal C_a$ is thin, there are no
  72: nontrivial coherence choices to make: functoriality of the object maps is
  73: the functoriality of $F_{p,s}$, and functoriality on automorphisms is the
  74: restriction functoriality of the sheaf $\Band$. The associated category
  75: over $\mathcal C_a$ is therefore fibred in groupoids. Its connected
  76: components over $U$ are exactly the objects $F_{p,s}(U)$, because
  77: distinct objects have no morphisms between them and each automorphism
  78: group is connected to its own object only. Hence
  79: \[
  80: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}})(U)\cong F_{p,s}(U)
  81: \]
  82: naturally in $U$.
  83: 
  84: For an object $\sigma\in F_{p,s}(U)$, the automorphism presheaf on
  85: $\mathcal C_a/U$ is
  86: \[
  87: V\longmapsto \operatorname{Aut}(\sigma|_V)=\Band(V),
  88: \]
  89: so its sheafification is already the restriction $\Band|_{\mathcal C_a/U}$.
  90: Thus $\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}}$ is a banded groupoid lift with band
  91: $\Band$.
  92: 
  93: It remains to prove the separatedness criterion. Suppose first that
  94: $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is separated on the slice. Let $\sigma,\tau\in
  95: F_{p,s}(U)$ and let $\{U_i\to U\}_{i\in I}$ be a cover. A matching
  96: family of local isomorphisms
  97: \[
  98: \alpha_i:\sigma|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau|_{U_i}
  99: \]
 100: can exist only when
 101: \[
 102: \sigma|_{U_i}=\tau|_{U_i}
 103: \qquad(\forall i).
 104: \]
 105: Separatedness on the slice then gives $\sigma=\tau$. The local isomorphisms
 106: $\alpha_i$ are therefore sections of the sheaf $\Band$ over the $U_i$,
 107: and their matching condition is exactly the sheaf matching condition for
 108: $\Band$. Since $\Band$ is a sheaf, the $\alpha_i$ glue uniquely to a
 109: global element of $\Band(U)$, hence to a unique global isomorphism
 110: $\sigma\to\tau$. Thus every isomorphism presheaf is a sheaf.
 111: 
 112: Conversely, assume that $\mathfrak P_r^{\mathrm{spl}}$ is separated. Let
 113: $\sigma,\tau\in F_{p,s}(U)$ have equal restrictions on a cover
 114: $\{U_i\to U\}_{i\in I}$. The identity element of $\Band(U_i)$ gives an
 115: isomorphism
 116: \[
 117: \sigma|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\sim}\tau|_{U_i}
 118: \]
 119: for every $i$, and these identities form a matching family. Separatedness
 120: of the isomorphism presheaf glues them to a global isomorphism
 121: $\sigma\to\tau$ in the split groupoid. Such a global isomorphism exists
 122: only when $\sigma=\tau$. Therefore $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is separated
 123: at every $U$, which is separatedness on the slice.
 124: \end{proof}
 125: 
 126: \begin{proposition}[Consequences of a chosen gluing-sensitive banded lift datum]\label{prop:gluing-sensitive-lift-consequences}
 127: Let $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ be admissible at a state $p$, and suppose $r$ is equipped with a gluing-sensitive banded lift datum in the sense of \Cref{def:gluing-sensitive-banded-lift}. Then the later component-obstruction theory depends on this extra datum, rather than on the split prestack of \Cref{prop:split-banded-groupoid-lift}, as follows:
 128: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 129: \item the sheafified global sections of $r$ are identified with $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)$ via \Cref{thm:visible-value-components};
 130: \item if the cofinal gerbe-adapted-cover hypothesis of
 131: \artresult{Component gerbes and component obstruction classes} is added,
 132: then each global component section determines a component gerbe as in
 133: \artresult{Supporting component gerbes of global component sections};
 134: \item if, in addition, the stackification map is essentially surjective on the terminal fibre and $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is separated when the matching-family predicate \(\CompSec_s\) is invoked, then the comparison between global sections and neutral component gerbes is exactly the one proved in
 135: \artresult{Global sections versus neutral component gerbes} and
 136: \artresult{Component-gerbe criterion for matching without amalgamation}.
 137: \end{enumerate}
 138: \end{proposition}
 139: 
 140: \begin{proof}
 141: Clause \textup{(ii)} of \Cref{def:gluing-sensitive-banded-lift} identifies the connected components of the chosen stack $\mathfrak L_r$ with the sheafification of the presheaf of local sections. Combined with \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}(ii), this is the stack-theoretic input used later to define global component sections and their component gerbes. Essential surjectivity on the terminal fibre supplies the comparison between stack-global sections and actual global lift objects via \Cref{prop:no-new-global-objects}. Finally, the acyclic cover data in \Cref{def:gluing-sensitive-banded-lift} are exactly the hypotheses invoked in \artresult{Component gerbes and component obstruction classes}. Together these assumptions are precisely what is needed for the later gerbe and obstruction arguments.
 142: \end{proof}
 143: 
 144: \begin{proposition}[Components of the stackification]\label{prop:stackification-components}
 145: In the setting of \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift} over the pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$, so without assuming an ambient meet-semilattice presentation, the sheaf of connected components of $\mathfrak L_r$ is canonically identified with the sheafification of the restricted presheaf of local sections $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$.
 146: \end{proposition}
 147: 
 148: \begin{proof}
 149: By stackification, the morphism presheaves of $\mathfrak P_r$ are sheafified and every object of $\mathfrak L_r$ is locally in the essential image of $\mathfrak P_r\to \mathfrak L_r$ \cite[Tag 02ZP]{StacksProject}. Passing to isomorphism classes therefore shows that $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)$ is the sheafification of $\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)$. Since stacks in setoids are precisely sheaves of sets \cite[Tag 042Y]{StacksProject}, we obtain
 150: \[
 151: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a\bigl(\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\bigr).
 152: \]
 153: Using \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}(i), this becomes
 154: \[
 155: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
 156: \qedhere
 157: \]
 158: \end{proof}
 159: 
 160: \begin{proposition}[Naturality of the stackification component map]\label{prop:stackification-component-unit-naturality}
 161: In the setting of \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}, the map of component
 162: sheaves induced by the stackification morphism
 163: \[
 164: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to a(\mathfrak P_r)=\mathfrak L_r
 165: \]
 166: is, under the identification
 167: \[
 168: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}
 169: \quad\text{and}\quad
 170: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a},
 171: \]
 172: exactly the sheafification unit
 173: \[
 174: u:F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\longrightarrow
 175: a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
 176: \]
 177: Equivalently, for every object \(U\) of the slice and every
 178: \(y\in\mathfrak P_r(U)\),
 179: \[
 180: [\iota_r(y)]=u_U([y])
 181: \]
 182: in \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(U)\).
 183: \end{proposition}
 184: 
 185: \begin{proof}
 186: \Cref{prop:stackification-components} identifies
 187: \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\) with the sheafification of
 188: \(\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\). This identification is induced by
 189: the universal morphism from a presheaf to its sheafification: applying
 190: \(\pi_0\) to \(\iota_r\) sends a prestack object to the same local
 191: component, now viewed in the sheaf of components. After the defining
 192: identification
 193: \(\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\), this
 194: universal morphism is precisely \(u\).
 195: \end{proof}
 196: 
 197: \begin{definition}[Essential surjectivity on the terminal fibre]\label{def:global-conservativity}
 198: In the setting of \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}, now over a pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$, let
 199: \[
 200: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to \mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)
 201: \]
 202: be the stackification morphism. We say that $\mathfrak P_r$ is \emph{essentially surjective on the terminal fibre over $a$} if every object of the terminal stack fibre $(\mathfrak L_r)_a$ is isomorphic to the image of some object of the terminal prestack fibre $(\mathfrak P_r)_a$; equivalently, the induced functor
 203: \[
 204: (\iota_r)_a:(\mathfrak P_r)_a\to (\mathfrak L_r)_a
 205: \]
 206: is essentially surjective.
 207: \end{definition}
 208: 
 209: \begin{definition}[Global representability of component sections]\label{def:terminal-component-effectivity}
 210: In the setting of \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}, the stackification
 211: $\mathfrak L_r$ has \emph{globally represented component sections} if the canonical
 212: map from isomorphism classes of terminal-fibre objects to terminal sections
 213: of the component sheaf,
 214: \[
 215: \pi_0\bigl(\mathfrak L_r(a)\bigr)\longrightarrow \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a),
 216: \]
 217: is surjective. Equivalently, every section of the sheaf of connected
 218: components over $a$ is represented by an actual object of
 219: $\mathfrak L_r(a)$.
 220: \end{definition}
 221: 
 222: \begin{proposition}[No new global objects at $a$]\label{prop:no-new-global-objects}
 223: Assume essential surjectivity on the terminal fibre over $a$ in the sense of \Cref{def:global-conservativity} for a banded groupoid lift on the pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$. Then
 224: \[
 225: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r)
 226: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 227: \mathfrak L_r(a)\neq\varnothing.
 228: \]
 229: \end{proposition}
 230: 
 231: \begin{proof}
 232: By \Cref{def:existence-levels} and \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift}(i),
 233: \[
 234: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r)
 235: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 236: F_{p,s}(a)\neq\varnothing
 237: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 238: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(a)\neq\varnothing
 239: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 240: (\mathfrak P_r)_a\neq\varnothing.
 241: \]
 242: If $(\mathfrak P_r)_a\neq\varnothing$, choose $y\in (\mathfrak P_r)_a$. Then $\iota_r(y)\in \mathfrak L_r(a)$, so $\mathfrak L_r(a)\neq\varnothing$. Conversely, if $x\in \mathfrak L_r(a)$, essential surjectivity of $(\iota_r)_a$ yields $y\in (\mathfrak P_r)_a$ with $\iota_r(y)\cong x$, so $(\mathfrak P_r)_a\neq\varnothing$.
 243: \end{proof}
 244: 
 245: \begin{definition}[Representative-rigid lift]\label{def:representative-rigid-lift}
 246: In the setting of \Cref{def:banded-groupoid-lift} over the pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$, a banded groupoid lift
 247: \[
 248: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_a
 249: \]
 250: is called \emph{representative-rigid} if for every object $U$ of $\mathcal C_a$ there is a map
 251: \[
 252: s_U:F_{p,s}(U)\to \Ob(\mathfrak P_r(U))
 253: \]
 254: such that:
 255: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 256: \item the image of $s_U(\sigma)$ in
 257: \[
 258: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(U)\cong F_{p,s}(U)
 259: \]
 260: is $\sigma$;
 261: \item for every morphism $f:V\to U$ in $\mathcal C_a$ and every $\sigma\in F_{p,s}(U)$,
 262: \[
 263: f^*s_U(\sigma)=s_V(\res_V^U(\sigma)).
 264: \]
 265: \end{enumerate}
 266: Here \(f^*\) is computed with respect to a fixed split cleavage of the
 267: fibred groupoid \(\mathfrak P_r\), so pullbacks compose on the nose. Thus
 268: the isomorphism classes of lift objects admit a strictly pullback-stable choice
 269: of representatives. This is the condition abbreviated by
 270: \emph{representative-rigid}. Without this split-cleavage convention the same condition
 271: would have to be stated with coherent cartesian isomorphisms, and the proof
 272: of descent below would require the corresponding triple-cocycle coherence
 273: instead of literal equalities of pulled-back representatives.
 274: \end{definition}
 275: 
  60: sheaf axiom at \(X\) gives an amalgamation \(\sigma\in F(X)\). The
  61: sections \(u_X(\sigma)\) and \(\xi\) restrict to the same sections of
  62: \(aF\) on the cover \(\{X_i\to X\}_i\), so separatedness of the sheaf
  63: \(aF\) gives \(u_X(\sigma)=\xi\). Hence \(u_X\) is surjective, and it is
  64: injective by the first paragraph.
  65: \end{proof}
  66: 
  67: \begin{lemma}[Isomorphism torsor for a lifted component]\label{lem:canonical-lift-isom-torsor}
  68: Let $\mathfrak P_r$ be a representative-rigid banded groupoid lift with band $\Band$ on $\mathcal C_a$, let
  69: \[
  70: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to\mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)
  71: \]
  72: be the stackification map, and let $x\in\mathfrak L_r(a)$ and $\sigma\in F_{p,s}(a)$ satisfy
  73: \[
  74: [\iota_r(s_a(\sigma))]=[x]
  75: \quad\text{in}\quad
  76: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a).
  77: \]
  78: Then the sheaf
  79: \[
  80: \mathcal I_{\sigma,x}:=
  81: \underline{\mathrm{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L_r}(\iota_r(s_a(\sigma)),x)
  82: \]
  83: is a $\Band$-torsor on $\mathcal C_a$. If $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0$, then $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ has a global section, equivalently
  84: \[
  85: \iota_r(s_a(\sigma))\cong x
  86: \quad\text{in}\quad
  87: \mathfrak L_r(a).
  88: \]
  89: \end{lemma}
  90: 
  91: \begin{proof}
  92: Write
  93: \[
  94: y:=s_a(\sigma).
  95: \]
  96: The equality of component classes in the sheaf $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)$ means precisely that the two objects $\iota_r(y)$ and $x$ become isomorphic after passing to a cover of $a$. Hence there is a covering family
  97: \[
  98: \{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I}
  99: \]
 100: and isomorphisms
 101: \[
 102: \phi_i:\iota_r(y)|_{U_i}\xrightarrow{\sim}x|_{U_i}.
 103: \]
 104: Thus $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ is locally nonempty.
 105: 
 106: For every object $U$ of $\mathcal C_a$ and every local isomorphism
 107: \[
 108: \phi:\iota_r(y)|_U\xrightarrow{\sim}x|_U,
 109: \]
 110: right composition gives a simply transitive action of
 111: \[
 112: \underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{\mathfrak L_r}(\iota_r(y)|_U)
 113: \]
 114: on $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}(U)$:
 115: \[
 116: \alpha\longmapsto \phi\circ\alpha.
 117: \]
 118: Because $\mathfrak L_r$ is the stackification of a $\Band$-banded prestack, the automorphism sheaf of $\iota_r(y)|_U$ is identified with $\Band|_U$; the band is abelian, so the same torsor is obtained from left composition through the automorphism sheaf of $x|_U$. Therefore $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ is a $\Band$-torsor. This is the usual Isom-torsor construction for a banded stack \cite[Chap.~III]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}, \cite[Tag 04TU]{StacksProject}.
 119: 
 120: The nonabelian cohomology set $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)$ classifies $\Band$-torsors on $\mathcal C_a$ up to isomorphism. Since $\Band$ is here an abelian sheaf, the distinguished point $0\in H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)$ is the class of the trivial torsor. If $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0$, every $\Band$-torsor is trivial; hence $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ is isomorphic to the trivial torsor $\Band$ and has a global section. Such a section is exactly an isomorphism $\iota_r(y)\cong x$ in the terminal fibre $\mathfrak L_r(a)$.
 121: \end{proof}

  96: The finite-group endpoint is correspondingly stated as a realization

 110: 
 111: \subsection*{Inputs, standard steps, and paper-specific steps}
 112: 
 113: The logical order of the construction is
 114: \[
 115: \begin{array}{c}
 116: (\mathcal C,F,\mathfrak P,\mathscr A)\\
 117: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize stackification}\\
 118: \mathfrak L,\ \pi_0(\mathfrak L)\cong aF\\
 119: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize pull back over }v\in(aF)(a)\\
 120: \mathfrak G_v,\ [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\\
 121: \big\downarrow\text{\scriptsize selected finite presentation and UCT}\\
 122: \operatorname{ev}_{\omega_v}:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A,\quad
 123: K_v,\quad Q_v.
 124: \end{array}
 125: \]
 126: The first line is supplied data.  The component gerbe in the third line is
 127: Giraud's standard maximal subgerbe over a component section.  Its neutrality
 128: criterion and transport under a banded equivalence are also standard.  The
 129: last line uses standard Cech comparison and UCT after a finite presentation
 130: has been selected.
 131: 
 132: The article-specific statements concern restrictions and simultaneous
 133: control across this sequence.  The rigidity theorem identifies a strict
 134: representative hypothesis that collapses a possible terminal gluing failure
 135: when \(H^1\) vanishes.  The prescribed-realization theorem constructs one
 136: prestack in which the component presheaf, terminal fibre, neutrality set, and
 137: finite family of UCT evaluations are fixed together.  The wedge result is
 138: then a corollary obtained by combining that construction with finite-group
 139: arithmetic.  It is not presented as a separate classification mechanism.
 140: 
 141: This division also explains the theorem count.  Component-gerbe existence,
 142: neutrality, banded naturality, UCT, duality, subgroup sums and intersections,
 143: and quotient-fibre cardinality are consolidated rather than restated as
 144: independent theorems.  Numbered statements are retained only where they
 145: serve one of three roles:
 146: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 147: \item record the standard component-gerbe package with its exact Giraud
 148:       antecedent;
 149: \item state a paper-specific comparison, rigidity, or simultaneous
 150:       realization assertion;
 151: \item isolate an application boundary or the wedge realization endpoint.
 152: \end{enumerate}
 153: 
 154: There are likewise two distinct uses of cohomology.  The Giraud class
 155: \[
 156:  [\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
 157: \]
 158: decides neutrality of the full component gerbe.  After a finite connected
 159: good-cover presentation with constant coefficient group \(A\) is fixed, its
 160: image under
 161: \[
 162:  H^2(N,A)\longrightarrow
 163:  \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)
 164: \]
 165: measures only evaluation on integral \(2\)-cycles.  A nonzero Ext class can
 166: be invisible to this map.  The homological image is therefore a quotient
 167: construction applied to a supplied gerbe class, not a complete obstruction
 168: theory for neutrality.
 169: 
 170: \subsection*{Scope and relation to the companion manuscript}
 171: 
 172: The lift data in this article are supplied.  They are not constructed from
 173: teams, logical models, or empirical probability tables.  A bare empirical
 174: model has a canonical split stack lift whose component gerbes are neutral.
 175: The Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction is instead a relative Cech
 176: \(H^1\)-torsor class indexed by a local section
 177: \cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.  The empty-domain argument
 178: in \Cref{thm:empirical-model-boundary} prevents a universal recovery of those
 179: classes from a global-component-section map.
 180: 
 181: The companion manuscript
 182: \cite{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure} uses meet-semilattice sites.  The
 183: overlap with the present pullback-site treatment is as follows.
 184: \begin{center}
 185: \begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.31\textwidth}

 204: 
 205: \subsection*{Organization}
 206: 
 207: Section~\ref{sec:preliminaries} fixes the minimal presheaf and prestack
 208: interface.  Section~\ref{sec:gerbe-obstruction} records the standard Giraud
 209: component package, proves the comparison criterion, and gives the full
 210: terminal-rigidity proof.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-visibility} proves the
 211: connected-overlap, basis-site, and derived comparisons before constructing
 212: the prescribed finite-label prestack.  Section~\ref{sec:homological-images}
 213: collects the UCT and aggregate consequences.  The wedge application and the
 214: empirical-model boundary occupy Sections~\ref{sec:wedge-application} and
 215: \ref{sec:empirical-boundary}.  The separately compiled technical appendix
 216: contains only presentation bookkeeping, extended examples, and the narrow
 217: lower-language boundary argument; the proofs needed to certify the principal
 218: claims are all contained here.

 590: 
 591: The gerbes in this proof are obtained from the standard Cech-cocycle
 592: construction.  What the theorem adds is the single finite-label prestack
 593: whose sheaf of components, terminal fibre, neutrality pattern, and homological
 594: images are all prescribed simultaneously.
 595: 
 596: \subsection*{The two terminal-fibre endpoints}
 597: 
 598: If every prescribed map is zero, then \(\Lambda_0=\Lambda\).  The component
 599: presheaf is already constant on every nonempty object, all summands are
 600: neutral, and the stackification has a terminal object in every global
 601: component.  The theorem reduces to a finite coproduct of split
 602: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stacks.  Matching families and global presheaf
 603: sections are both indexed by \(\Lambda\).
 604: 
 605: At the other endpoint, suppose every prescribed map is nonzero.  Then
 606: \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\), so
 607: \[
 608:  F(a)=\varnothing,\qquad
 609:  (aF)(a)=\Lambda,\qquad
 610:  \mathfrak P(a)=\varnothing.
 611: \]
 612: Every global component gerbe is non-neutral, hence
 613: \(\mathfrak L(a)=\varnothing\) as well.  The map
 614: \[
 615:  \mathfrak P(a)\longrightarrow\mathfrak L(a)
 616: \]
 617: is essentially surjective because both fibres are empty.  This is the
 618: vacuous terminal-essential-surjectivity case used by matching without
 619: amalgamation.  It is not a claim that the non-neutral global component
 620: sections have terminal objects; they do not.
 621: 
 622: For a mixed family, \(\mathfrak L(a)\) contains objects exactly in the
 623: components indexed by \(\Lambda_0\).  The nonzero labels still occur as
 624: sections of \(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\cong(aF)(a)\), and their full component
 625: substacks are locally inhabited gerbes without terminal objects.  This is
 626: the concrete distinction among the three terminal levels in
 627: \eqref{eq:three-terminal-levels}.
 628: 
 629: \subsection*{A three-label calculation}
 630: 
 631: Take a triangulated \(2\)-sphere and an open-star cover of a barycentric
 632: subdivision.  Its nerve \(N\) satisfies
 633: \[
 634:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,\qquad H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z.
 635: \]
 636: Let \(A=\mathbb Z/6\mathbb Z\), and take the label set
 637: \[
 638:  \Lambda=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}.
 639: \]
 640: Prescribe
 641: \[
 642: \begin{aligned}
 643:  \phi_{\mathtt n}(1)&=0,\\
 644:  \phi_{\mathtt p}(1)&=2,\\
 645:  \phi_{\mathtt q}(1)&=3.
 646: \end{aligned}
 647: \]
 648: Then
 649: \[
 650:  \Lambda_0=\{\mathtt n\},\qquad
 651:  K_{\mathtt n}=0,\qquad
 652:  K_{\mathtt p}=\langle2\rangle,\qquad
 653:  K_{\mathtt q}=\langle3\rangle.
 654: \]
 655: The three image groups have orders \(1,3,2\), respectively.  The UCT
 656: isomorphism chooses three classes in \(H^2(N,A)\cong A\), and normalized
 657: cocycles \(\alpha_{\mathtt n}=0\),
 658: \(\alpha_{\mathtt p}\), and \(\alpha_{\mathtt q}\) feed the twisted
 659: composition formula.
 660: 
 661: The component presheaf has
 662: \[
 663:  F(a)=\{\mathtt n\},
 664:  \qquad
 665:  F(U_J)=\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\}
 666: \]
 667: on every proper nonempty intersection.  Its sheafification has three
 668: terminal component sections.  Only the \(\mathtt n\)-component has a
 669: terminal stack object.  Therefore
 670: \[
 671:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)
 672:  =\{\mathtt n,\mathtt p,\mathtt q\},
 673:  \qquad
 674:  \pi_0(\mathfrak L(a))=\{\mathtt n\}.
 675: \]
 676: The notation on the right means the set of isomorphism classes of actual
 677: terminal objects; it is smaller than the value at \(a\) of the component
 678: sheaf.
 679: 
 680: For the two nonzero labels,
 681: \[
 682:  Q_{\mathtt p}=A/\langle2\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z,
 683:  \qquad
 684:  Q_{\mathtt q}=A/\langle3\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/3\mathbb Z.
 685: \]
 686: Their images are complementary:
 687: \[
 688:  \langle2\rangle\cap\langle3\rangle=0,
 689:  \qquad
 690:  \langle2\rangle+\langle3\rangle=A.
 691: \]
 692: If all three labels are aggregated, the zero image from
 693: \(\mathtt n\) keeps the intersection equal to zero, while the sum remains
 694: \(A\).  Hence
 695: \[
 696:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}=A,\qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0.
 697: \]
 698: 
 699: This mixed example does not satisfy matching without amalgamation because
 700: \(F(a)\ne\varnothing\).  It does contain matching families with labels
 701: \(\mathtt p\) and \(\mathtt q\) which individually have no amalgamation.
 702: Deleting the neutral label produces the all-nonzero endpoint above: the same
 703: two component classes remain, \(F(a)\) becomes empty, and the existence
 704: predicate \(\operatorname{MWA}(F,a)\) becomes true.

  55:  \frac{K_1+K_2}{K_1\cap K_2}.
  56: \]
  57: We ask when the selected realization construction makes this kernel equal to
  58: the band itself by requiring
  59: \begin{equation}\label{eq:complementary-images}
  60:  K_1\cap K_2=0,
  61:  \qquad K_1+K_2=G,
  62:  \qquad K_1,K_2\ne0.
  63: \end{equation}
  64: 
  65: \begin{corollary}[Two-component realization on a wedge of \(2\)-spheres]
  66: \label{thm:wedge-sphere-two-component-label-loss}
  67: Let \(G\) be a finite abelian group and let \(d(G)\) be its minimal number
  68: of generators.  The following are equivalent:
  69: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  70: \item \(G\ne0\), \(d(G)\le2\beta\), and \(G\) is not cyclic of
  71:       prime-power order;
  72: \item on one of the selected finite open-star covers in
  73:       \(\mathcal B_\beta\), the prestack construction of
  74:       \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} has two non-neutral
  75:       component gerbes whose homological images satisfy
  76:       \eqref{eq:complementary-images}.
  77: \end{enumerate}
  78: When these conditions hold,
  79: \[
  80:  Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\cong G,
  81:  \qquad Q_{\mathrm{com}}=0,
  82:  \qquad
  83:  \ker(Q_{\mathrm{lab}}\to Q_{\mathrm{com}})\cong G,
  84: \]
  85: and the constructed component presheaf has matching without amalgamation.
  86: The assertion is relative to this selected finite-site realization; it is not
  87: a presentation-independent classification of gerbes or finite abelian groups.
  88: \end{corollary}
  89: 
  90: \begin{proof}
  91: For every selected cover, the nerve is a barycentric subdivision of
  92: \(K_\beta\).  The nerve and basis-site comparisons of
  93: \Cref{prop:finite-site-comparison} give
  94: \[
  95:  H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
  96:  \qquad
  97:  H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta,
  98: \]
  99: and identify the finite Cech representatives with the corresponding derived
 100: classes on \(\mathcal B_\beta\).
 101: 
 102: Assume (ii).  By \Cref{prop:uct-aggregate-package}(iv), each \(K_i\) is
 103: generated by at most \(\beta\) elements.  The complementary-image equations
 104: give
 105: \[
 106:  G=K_1\oplus K_2.
 107: \]
 108: Hence \(d(G)\le d(K_1)+d(K_2)\le2\beta\).  Since both summands are nonzero,
 109: \(G\) is decomposable.  The indecomposable finite abelian groups are exactly
 110: the cyclic groups of prime-power order \cite[Chap.~12]{DummitFoote2004}, so
 111: (i) follows.
 112: 
 113: For completeness, the generator inequality may be checked prime by prime.
 114: For a finite abelian group \(H\), let
 115: \[
 116:  r_p(H)=\dim_{\mathbb F_p}(H/pH).
 117: \]
 118: Then \(d(H)=\max_p r_p(H)\).  Direct sums give
 119: \[
 120:  r_p(K_1\oplus K_2)=r_p(K_1)+r_p(K_2),
 121: \]
 122: and hence
 123: \[
 124:  d(G)
 125:  =\max_p\bigl(r_p(K_1)+r_p(K_2)\bigr)
 126:  \le d(K_1)+d(K_2)
 127:  \le2\beta.
 128: \]
 129: This proves the numerical necessity without choosing an invariant-factor
 130: basis.
 131: 
 132: Conversely, suppose (i).  If \(d(G)=r\ge2\), choose an invariant-factor
 133: decomposition
 134: \[
 135:  G\cong C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_r},
 136:  \qquad n_1\mid n_2\mid\cdots\mid n_r,
 137: \]
 138: and let \(t=\min\{\beta,r-1\}\).  Put
 139: \[
 140:  K_1=C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_t},
 141:  \qquad
 142:  K_2=C_{n_{t+1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_r}.
 143: \]
 144: Both groups are nonzero.  We have \(d(K_1)\le\beta\), while
 145: \(d(K_2)=r-t\le\beta\) because \(r\le2\beta\).
 146: 
 147: If \(d(G)=1\), then \(G\) is cyclic.  Its order is not a prime power, so it
 148: has coprime factors \(a,b>1\).  The Chinese remainder theorem gives
 149: \[
 150:  G\cong C_a\oplus C_b.
 151: \]
 152: Taking \(K_1=C_a\) and \(K_2=C_b\) again gives two nonzero summands, each
 153: generated by one element.  Thus in either case
 154: \(G=K_1\oplus K_2\), with \(d(K_i)\le\beta\).
 155: 
 156: In the case \(r\ge2\), the chosen invariant-factor decomposition makes the
 157: generator counts transparent.  The first block has \(t\le\beta\) cyclic
 158: factors.  If \(r\le\beta\), then \(t=r-1\) and the second block is cyclic.
 159: If \(r>\beta\), then \(t=\beta\) and
 160: \[
 161:  r-t=r-\beta\le\beta
 162: \]
 163: because \(r=d(G)\le2\beta\).  Thus neither block is empty and both satisfy
 164: the rank constraint required by the UCT package.
 165: 
 166: In the cyclic case, write
 167: \[
 168:  |G|=\prod_{p\mid |G|}p^{e_p}
 169: \]
 170: with at least two distinct primes.  Partition the prime set into two
 171: nonempty subsets \(P_1,P_2\), put
 172: \[
 173:  a=\prod_{p\in P_1}p^{e_p},
 174:  \qquad
 175:  b=\prod_{p\in P_2}p^{e_p},
 176: \]
 177: and use \(C_{ab}\cong C_a\oplus C_b\).  This gives the required summands
 178: without assuming a particular factorization in advance.
 179: 
 180: Choose homomorphisms
 181: \[
 182:  \phi_i:\mathbb Z^\beta\longrightarrow G
 183: \]
 184: with images \(K_i\), and transport them across
 185: \(H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\).  The finite-site realization
 186: theorem, with label set \(\{1,2\}\), produces the two component gerbes and
 187: their prescribed images.  Both maps are nonzero, so both gerbes are
 188: non-neutral and the component presheaf has matching without amalgamation.
 189: Finally, \eqref{eq:aggregate-quotients} and
 190: \eqref{eq:aggregate-exact-sequence} give the three displayed quotient
 191: identities.
 192: 
 193: To make the maps explicit, choose generators
 194: \[
 195:  K_i=\langle k_{i1},\dots,k_{i r_i}\rangle,
 196:  \qquad r_i\le\beta,
 197: \]
 198: and send the first \(r_i\) standard basis vectors of \(\mathbb Z^\beta\)
 199: to the displayed generators, sending the remaining basis vectors to zero.
 200: The UCT isomorphism selects classes \(\omega_i\in H^2(N,G)\), and the
 201: construction of
 202: \Cref{thm:prescribed-component-image-construction} selects normalized
 203: cocycles and places them in the two labelled summands of one prestack.
 204: Since \(K_i\ne0\), neither evaluation map is zero, so both Giraud classes are
 205: nonzero in the \(H_1=0\) regime.
 206: \end{proof}
 207: 
 208: For \(\beta=1\), the statement says that a nonzero finite abelian group is
 209: attained in this two-component manner precisely when it splits as a direct
 210: sum of two nonzero cyclic groups.  This includes cyclic groups whose orders
 211: have at least two distinct prime divisors and noncyclic two-generated groups,
 212: but excludes every cyclic \(p\)-group.  This small case illustrates that the
 213: criterion is the sharp arithmetic endpoint of the chosen realization, not a
 214: new structural theorem about finite abelian groups.
 215: 
 216: \subsection*{Low-rank cases}
 217: 
 218: For \(\beta=1\), both images must be cyclic.  The admissible groups fall into
 219: two families.
 220: 
 221: First, a cyclic group \(C_n\) is admissible exactly when \(n\) has at least
 222: two distinct prime divisors.  If \(n=ab\) with \(\gcd(a,b)=1\) and
 223: \(a,b>1\), take
 224: \[
 225:  K_1=\langle b\rangle\cong C_a,
 226:  \qquad
 227:  K_2=\langle a\rangle\cong C_b
 228: \]
 229: inside \(C_{ab}\).  They have zero intersection and generate the group.
 230: If \(n=p^e\), the subgroup lattice is a chain and no two nonzero subgroups
 231: have zero intersection.
 232: 
 233: Second, a noncyclic two-generated group is admissible whenever it decomposes
 234: as a direct sum of two nonzero cyclic groups.  For example,
 235: \[
 236:  G=C_4\oplus C_2,\qquad
 237:  K_1=C_4\oplus0,\qquad K_2=0\oplus C_2.
 238: \]
 239: Each image is generated by one element of \(H_2\cong\mathbb Z\).  On the
 240: other hand, \(C_2^3\) requires three generators and violates
 241: \(d(G)\le2\), even though it has many complementary subgroups.
 242: 
 243: For \(\beta=2\), groups with at most four generators are permitted, provided
 244: they are not cyclic of prime-power order.  For instance,
 245: \[
 246:  G=C_2^4
 247: \]
 248: is realized by the complementary rank-two subgroups
 249: \[
 250:  K_1=\langle e_1,e_2\rangle,
 251:  \qquad
 252:  K_2=\langle e_3,e_4\rangle.
 253: \]
 254: Each is the image of \(\mathbb Z^2\).  The bound is sharp:
 255: \(C_2^5\) cannot be written as a sum of two subgroups each generated by at
 256: most two elements, since their sum would have rank at most four.
 257: 
 258: Mixed-primary examples do not require the same decomposition at each prime.
 259: Take
 260: \[
 261:  G=C_4\oplus C_2\oplus C_3.
 262: \]
 263: For \(\beta=1\), the decomposition
 264: \[
 265:  K_1=C_4,\qquad K_2=C_2\oplus C_3\cong C_6
 266: \]
 267: has two cyclic summands, so the group is already admissible.  This illustrates
 268: why \(d(G)\) is the maximum primary rank rather than the total number of
 269: cyclic factors appearing in an arbitrary decomposition.
 270: 
 271: In every example, the conclusion remains about the selected prestack
 272: realization.  The arithmetic chooses possible image subgroups; the
 273: finite-site theorem supplies component gerbes having those images.  Neither
 274: step classifies all gerbes on the wedge or assigns such data to an unrelated
 275: semantic model.
 276:

I’m also tightening the finite-site proof at the exact sentence the report flagged: global isomorphism in a neutral component will be deduced from degree-one UCT and triviality of the Isom torsor, not merely from the gerbe axiom.

+\subsection*{The terminal torsor obstruction}
+
+Let \(\iota:\mathfrak P\to\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\) be the
+stackification of an \(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack.  For a global component
+section \(\lambda\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\), write
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-source-component-fibre}
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ :=\{p\in\mathfrak P(a):[\iota(p)]=\lambda\}
+\end{equation}
+for the full subgroupoid.  Thus ``in the component \(\lambda\)'' means all
+source objects whose images have sheaf-component \(\lambda\).  It does not
+mean one selected source component when several source components acquire
+the same component after sheafification.
+
+\begin{theorem}[Terminal torsor obstruction]
+\label{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction}
+Let \(\lambda\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\) be neutral.  If the restriction
+\[
+ \iota_\lambda:\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ \longrightarrow \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)
+\]
+is essentially surjective, then it is an equivalence of groupoids.
+Consequently,
+\[
+ \pi_0\bigl(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\bigr)
+\]
+is a principal homogeneous set under \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\), and
+for every \(p\in\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\),
+\[
+ \operatorname{Aut}_{\mathfrak P(a)}(p)
+ \cong H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A).
+\]
+In particular, if \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\) has one isomorphism class, then
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\).
+\end{theorem}
+
+\begin{proof}
+For \(p,q\in\mathfrak P(a)\), the presheaf
+\[
+ U\longmapsto
+ \operatorname{Isom}_{\mathfrak P(U)}(p|_U,q|_U)
+\]
+on \(\mathcal C/a\) is already a sheaf because \(\mathfrak P\) is a
+prestack.  Stackification replaces an isomorphism presheaf by its
+sheafification and therefore induces an isomorphism
+\[
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak P}(p,q)
+ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}
+   (\iota(p),\iota(q)).
+\]
+Taking sections over \(a\) shows that
+\(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is fully faithful
+\cite[Tag 02ZP]{StacksProject}.  Its restriction to the full subgroupoid in
+\eqref{eq:full-source-component-fibre} is therefore fully faithful, and the
+assumed essential surjectivity makes \(\iota_\lambda\) an equivalence.
+
+Choose a neutralizing object \(x\in\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\).  The standard
+contracted-product equivalence
+\[
+ \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\simeq
+ \operatorname{Tors}_{\mathscr A}(\mathcal C),
+ \qquad
+ y\longmapsto
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}(x,y),
+\]
+has quasi-inverse \(T\mapsto x\wedge^{\mathscr A}T\)
+\cite[Chap.~IV]{Giraud1971}.  Isomorphism classes of
+\(\mathscr A\)-torsors form \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\), so the
+isomorphism classes of objects of the neutral gerbe form a principal
+homogeneous set under that group.  The fixed band identifies the
+automorphism sheaf of every object with \(\mathscr A\), whence its group of
+terminal automorphisms is
+\(\Gamma(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).  Transporting
+these statements across \(\iota_\lambda\) proves the assertions for
+\(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\).  A principal homogeneous set for a group can be
+a singleton only when the group is trivial.
+\end{proof}
+
+The fixed band in \Cref{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction} is essential.  If
+the band is not fixed, automorphisms of the coefficient sheaf can also act
+on the classification.
+
+\begin{theorem}[Essentially surjective marked terminal form]
+\label{thm:marked-terminal-torsor-form}
+Let a finite marking set \(\Lambda\) be identified with
+\(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\), and put
+\[
+ \Lambda_0
+ :=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\ne\varnothing\}.
+\]
+Assume that \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially
+surjective.  Then the component of the image induces a marking map
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-map}
+ m:\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)\longrightarrow\Lambda_0
+\end{equation}
+such that, for every \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the fibre
+\(m^{-1}(\lambda)\) is a principal homogeneous set under
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).
+
+More precisely, after choosing one neutralizing object in each neutral
+component,
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-groupoid-form}
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ \simeq \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)
+ \simeq \operatorname{Tors}_{\mathscr A}(\mathcal C)
+ \qquad(\lambda\in\Lambda_0),
+\end{equation}
+\[
+ \pi_0\bigl(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\bigr)
+ \text{ is an }H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\text{-torsor},
+ \qquad
+ \operatorname{Aut}(p)\cong H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
+\]
+for every object \(p\) in that fibre.  For
+\(\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_0\),
+\[
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)=\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)=\varnothing.
+\]
+Consequently the complete set of terminal prestack isomorphism classes has
+the form
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-disjoint-union}
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)
+ \cong\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_0}T_\lambda,
+\end{equation}
+where every \(T_\lambda\) is an
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\)-torsor.
+\end{theorem}
+
+\begin{proof}
+Every \(p\in\mathfrak P(a)\) maps to an actual object of
+\(\mathfrak L(a)\), so its component label is neutral.  This defines
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-map}.  Essential surjectivity of
+\(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) restricts to essential surjectivity in
+every neutral component.  Apply
+\Cref{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction} componentwise to obtain
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-groupoid-form}, the descriptions of isomorphism
+classes and automorphisms, and the torsor fibres of \(m\).  A non-neutral
+component has no terminal stack object by definition; it can contain no
+image of a terminal prestack object either.  Taking isomorphism classes of
+the resulting disjoint union of full subgroupoids gives
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-disjoint-union}.
+\end{proof}
+
+\begin{remark}[The pointed-atlas alternative]
+\label{rem:pointed-atlas-terminal-form}
+There is a different consistent formulation, but it is not combined with
+\Cref{thm:marked-terminal-torsor-form}.  If one keeps
+\[
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)\cong\Lambda_0,
+\]
+then terminal essential surjectivity must in general be dropped.  The
+replacement is only
+\[
+ \operatorname{im}\!\left(
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)
+ \longrightarrow \pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)
+ \right)=\Lambda_0.
+\]
+This pointed-atlas, or chosen-neutralization, formulation supplies one
+distinguished neutralizing isomorphism class in every neutral marked
+component and none in a non-neutral component.  It omits the other global
+torsor twists that appear in the stackification, and is therefore weaker and
+more presentation-relative.  When \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\), even
+the distinguished isomorphism class is noncanonical; when \(H^1=0\), its
+representing object can still have the nontrivial automorphism group \(H^0\).
+If \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\) and
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\), there is no third formulation retaining
+simultaneously this singleton-per-label equality, terminal essential
+surjectivity, and the nonvanishing degree-one ambiguity.
+\end{remark}
+
+\begin{remark}[The coefficient-dependent threshold]
+\label{rem:terminal-torsor-coefficient-threshold}
+For a connected nerve \(N\), degree-one UCT gives
+\[
+ H^1(N;A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A).
+\]
+Thus \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) is sufficient to collapse the terminal torsor,
+but the coefficient-dependent condition
+\(\operatorname{Hom}(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)=0\) is weaker.  As far as the
+terminal clauses are concerned, the manuscript's integral
+\(H_1\)-vanishing hypothesis can therefore sometimes be relaxed while the
+singleton-per-neutral-label description and essential surjectivity are both
+retained.  They cannot both be retained when
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\) and at least one neutral label exists.
+The all-non-neutral case \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\) is unaffected:
+\(\mathfrak P(a)=\mathfrak L(a)=\varnothing\), and terminal essential
+surjectivity is vacuous.
+\end{remark}
+
+The two preceding theorems are consequences of standard stackification and
+torsor theory.  Their purpose is to identify exactly the unavoidable
+\(H^1\) ambiguity in terminal isomorphism classes and the \(H^0\) ambiguity
+in terminal automorphisms.  They are not a localization theorem.  A
+presentation-independent result over arbitrary cover presentations would
+additionally require a full refinement localization and a proof of the
+resulting biequivalence; no such claim is made here.
+
 \begin{theorem}[Presheaf-to-component-gerbe comparison criterion]
 \label{thm:gerbe-null-semantics}

 \Cref{prop:standard-component-gerbe-package}(iii) literal on a common
 presentation.
 
+\subsection*{The terminal torsor obstruction}
+
+Let \(\iota:\mathfrak P\to\mathfrak L=a(\mathfrak P)\) be the
+stackification of an \(\mathscr A\)-banded prestack.  For a global component
+section \(\lambda\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\), write
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-source-component-fibre}
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ :=\{p\in\mathfrak P(a):[\iota(p)]=\lambda\}
+\end{equation}
+for the full subgroupoid.  Thus ``in the component \(\lambda\)'' means all
+source objects whose images have sheaf-component \(\lambda\).  It does not
+mean one selected source component when several source components acquire
+the same component after sheafification.
+
+\begin{theorem}[Terminal torsor obstruction]
+\label{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction}
+Let \(\lambda\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\) be neutral.  If the restriction
+\[
+ \iota_\lambda:\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ \longrightarrow \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)
+\]
+is essentially surjective, then it is an equivalence of groupoids.
+Consequently,
+\[
+ \pi_0\bigl(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\bigr)
+\]
+is a principal homogeneous set under \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\), and
+for every \(p\in\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\),
+\[
+ \operatorname{Aut}_{\mathfrak P(a)}(p)
+ \cong H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A).
+\]
+In particular, if \(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\) has one isomorphism class, then
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=0\).
+\end{theorem}
+
+\begin{proof}
+For \(p,q\in\mathfrak P(a)\), the presheaf
+\[
+ U\longmapsto
+ \operatorname{Isom}_{\mathfrak P(U)}(p|_U,q|_U)
+\]
+on \(\mathcal C/a\) is already a sheaf because \(\mathfrak P\) is a
+prestack.  Stackification replaces an isomorphism presheaf by its
+sheafification and therefore induces an isomorphism
+\[
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak P}(p,q)
+ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}
+   (\iota(p),\iota(q)).
+\]
+Taking sections over \(a\) shows that
+\(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is fully faithful
+\cite[Tag 02ZP]{StacksProject}.  Its restriction to the full subgroupoid in
+\eqref{eq:full-source-component-fibre} is therefore fully faithful, and the
+assumed essential surjectivity makes \(\iota_\lambda\) an equivalence.
+
+Choose a neutralizing object \(x\in\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\).  The standard
+contracted-product equivalence
+\[
+ \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\simeq
+ \operatorname{Tors}_{\mathscr A}(\mathcal C),
+ \qquad
+ y\longmapsto
+ \underline{\operatorname{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L}(x,y),
+\]
+has quasi-inverse \(T\mapsto x\wedge^{\mathscr A}T\)
+\cite[Chap.~IV]{Giraud1971}.  Isomorphism classes of
+\(\mathscr A\)-torsors form \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\), so the
+isomorphism classes of objects of the neutral gerbe form a principal
+homogeneous set under that group.  The fixed band identifies the
+automorphism sheaf of every object with \(\mathscr A\), whence its group of
+terminal automorphisms is
+\(\Gamma(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)=H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).  Transporting
+these statements across \(\iota_\lambda\) proves the assertions for
+\(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\).  A principal homogeneous set for a group can be
+a singleton only when the group is trivial.
+\end{proof}
+
+The fixed band in \Cref{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction} is essential.  If
+the band is not fixed, automorphisms of the coefficient sheaf can also act
+on the classification.
+
+\begin{theorem}[Essentially surjective marked terminal form]
+\label{thm:marked-terminal-torsor-form}
+Let a finite marking set \(\Lambda\) be identified with
+\(\pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)\), and put
+\[
+ \Lambda_0
+ :=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)\ne\varnothing\}.
+\]
+Assume that \(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) is essentially
+surjective.  Then the component of the image induces a marking map
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-map}
+ m:\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)\longrightarrow\Lambda_0
+\end{equation}
+such that, for every \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), the fibre
+\(m^{-1}(\lambda)\) is a principal homogeneous set under
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\).
+
+More precisely, after choosing one neutralizing object in each neutral
+component,
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-groupoid-form}
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)
+ \simeq \mathfrak L[\lambda](a)
+ \simeq \operatorname{Tors}_{\mathscr A}(\mathcal C)
+ \qquad(\lambda\in\Lambda_0),
+\end{equation}
+\[
+ \pi_0\bigl(\mathfrak P_\lambda(a)\bigr)
+ \text{ is an }H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\text{-torsor},
+ \qquad
+ \operatorname{Aut}(p)\cong H^0(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)
+\]
+for every object \(p\) in that fibre.  For
+\(\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_0\),
+\[
+ \mathfrak P_\lambda(a)=\mathfrak L[\lambda](a)=\varnothing.
+\]
+Consequently the complete set of terminal prestack isomorphism classes has
+the form
+\begin{equation}\label{eq:marked-terminal-disjoint-union}
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)
+ \cong\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda_0}T_\lambda,
+\end{equation}
+where every \(T_\lambda\) is an
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\)-torsor.
+\end{theorem}
+
+\begin{proof}
+Every \(p\in\mathfrak P(a)\) maps to an actual object of
+\(\mathfrak L(a)\), so its component label is neutral.  This defines
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-map}.  Essential surjectivity of
+\(\mathfrak P(a)\to\mathfrak L(a)\) restricts to essential surjectivity in
+every neutral component.  Apply
+\Cref{thm:terminal-torsor-obstruction} componentwise to obtain
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-groupoid-form}, the descriptions of isomorphism
+classes and automorphisms, and the torsor fibres of \(m\).  A non-neutral
+component has no terminal stack object by definition; it can contain no
+image of a terminal prestack object either.  Taking isomorphism classes of
+the resulting disjoint union of full subgroupoids gives
+\eqref{eq:marked-terminal-disjoint-union}.
+\end{proof}
+
+\begin{remark}[The pointed-atlas alternative]
+\label{rem:pointed-atlas-terminal-form}
+There is a different consistent formulation, but it is not combined with
+\Cref{thm:marked-terminal-torsor-form}.  If one keeps
+\[
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)\cong\Lambda_0,
+\]
+then terminal essential surjectivity must in general be dropped.  The
+replacement is only
+\[
+ \operatorname{im}\!\left(
+ \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P)(a)
+ \longrightarrow \pi_0(\mathfrak L)(a)
+ \right)=\Lambda_0.
+\]
+This pointed-atlas, or chosen-neutralization, formulation supplies one
+distinguished neutralizing isomorphism class in every neutral marked
+component and none in a non-neutral component.  It omits the other global
+torsor twists that appear in the stackification, and is therefore weaker and
+more presentation-relative.  When \(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\), even
+the distinguished isomorphism class is noncanonical; when \(H^1=0\), its
+representing object can still have the nontrivial automorphism group \(H^0\).
+If \(\Lambda_0\ne\varnothing\) and
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\), there is no third formulation retaining
+simultaneously this singleton-per-label equality, terminal essential
+surjectivity, and the nonvanishing degree-one ambiguity.
+\end{remark}
+
+\begin{remark}[The coefficient-dependent threshold]
+\label{rem:terminal-torsor-coefficient-threshold}
+For a connected nerve \(N\), degree-one UCT gives
+\[
+ H^1(N;A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A).
+\]
+Thus \(H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0\) is sufficient to collapse the terminal torsor,
+but the coefficient-dependent condition
+\(\operatorname{Hom}(H_1(N,\mathbb Z),A)=0\) is weaker.  As far as the
+terminal clauses are concerned, the manuscript's integral
+\(H_1\)-vanishing hypothesis can therefore sometimes be relaxed while the
+singleton-per-neutral-label description and essential surjectivity are both
+retained.  They cannot both be retained when
+\(H^1(\mathcal C,\mathscr A)\ne0\) and at least one neutral label exists.
+The all-non-neutral case \(\Lambda_0=\varnothing\) is unaffected:
+\(\mathfrak P(a)=\mathfrak L(a)=\varnothing\), and terminal essential
+surjectivity is vacuous.
+\end{remark}
+
+The two preceding theorems are consequences of standard stackification and
+torsor theory.  Their purpose is to identify exactly the unavoidable
+\(H^1\) ambiguity in terminal isomorphism classes and the \(H^0\) ambiguity
+in terminal automorphisms.  They are not a localization theorem.  A
+presentation-independent result over arbitrary cover presentations would
+additionally require a full refinement localization and a proof of the
+resulting biequivalence; no such claim is made here.
